EXECUTIVE SUMMARY  
ES-1 
Executive Summary 
The City of Oceanside (City) is situated in the northwest corner of San Diego County and embodies 
a character that is unique to the region. With Camp Pendleton to the north, the Pacific Ocean to 
west, and the distinctly divergent cities of Vista and Carlsbad to the east and south, respectively; 
Oceanside stands out as a unique coastal community that maintains a working-class character while 
offering tourism and economic opportunities. Oceanside functions as both a suburb for regional 
employment centers, and its own economic hub of tourism, retail and restaurants, light 
manufacturing, and biotechnology sectors, to name a few. The developable land area of Oceanside 
is largely built out; however, the future holds tremendous opportunities for creating connectivity 
and efficiency in land uses, transportation, and energy systems.  
Oceanside, like its neighboring cities, is already being 
affected by climate change. The City’s coastal location 
presents additional challenges resulting from increased 
sea-level rise and associated coastal hazards such as 
beach 
erosion, 
and 
water 
quality 
and 
habitat 
degradation. While climate change is a global issue, steps 
can be taken to mitigate the effects of climate change at 
the regional and local level by implementing actions to 
reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions locally. The San Diego Association of Governments 
(SANDAG) leads the Regional Climate Action Planning Framework (ReCAP) to provide a 
framework for individual jurisdictions within the San Diego region to develop their own Climate 
Action Plans (CAPs). By establishing a regional framework, ReCAP promotes a consistent approach 
to emissions reduction across the entire region, ultimately contributing to the statewide goals. The 
City of Oceanside recognizes its role in the regional effort and has prepared this Climate Action 
Plan Update (CAP Update) to outline its strategies and commitments for reducing GHG emissions 
while prioritizing quality of life and sustainable economic development in Oceanside.  
 
ReCAP identifies best practices and 
guidance 
for 
preparing 
Climate 
Action Plans (CAPs) for the San 
Diego 
region 
and 
assists 
in 
monitoring implementation of CAPs. 
Sprinter light rail 


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
ES-2 
This CAP Update is occurring simultaneously with the 
second phase of the City’s General Plan Update (GPU), 
publicly branded as the “Onward Oceanside” project. 
While the GPU focuses on infill development in priority 
areas, the CAP Update builds on the commitments of 
the City’s 2019 CAP which resulted in several key 
actions for Oceanside such as joining the Clean Energy 
Alliance (CEA), installation of renewable energy 
facilities, adoption of the Zero Waste Plan 2020 
Update, among others. 
This CAP Update serves to consolidate the General Plan Update goals with ongoing sustainability 
initiatives to modernize transportation systems, development patterns, and buildings such that 
future growth in Oceanside is both sustainable and cost-effective for community members.  
COMPONENTS OF THE CAP UPDATE 
The CAP Update consists of several components including a GHG 
emissions analysis, GHG reduction targets, GHG reduction 
measures and associated actions, and procedures for monitoring 
and reporting progress on the implementation of GHG reduction 
measures. Figure ES-1 provides a graphical summary of the CAP 
Update components which are described in the sections below. 
These core components of the CAP Update allow for data-based 
policy decisions that are tailored to the community character and 
priorities of Oceanside. The CAP is structured with adaptive 
management in mind and will evolve with future updates to reflect 
revisions to City policies, advances in technology, regional 
development and economical trends and legislative reforms.

Greenhouse Gas Emissions Analysis 
The first step in the GHG emissions for the CAP Update includes developing a communitywide GHG 
emissions inventory to reflect baseline conditions in the City. This is followed by a forecast of 
emissions for select milestone years to study the trajectory of emissions associated with 
anticipated growth in the City. The baseline inventory and forecasts provide an understanding of 
the GHG emissions generating activities that occur in Oceanside and how they are expected to 
change in the future with the impacts of policies and legislation already in place. The baseline 
inventory was developed using 2018 data as it was most recent year jurisdictional transportation 
data were available prior to the COVID-19 pandemic which altered travel patterns. The GHG 
analysis also provides insight into policy levers that may affect GHG emissions and where mitigation 
efforts can be more impactful.  
The results of the GHG emissions analysis are summarized below:  
• 
Activities in the City generated approximately 845,000 metric tons of carbon dioxide 
equivalent (MTCO2e) in 2018. The term CO2e accounts for contributions from carbon dioxide, 
methane and nitrous oxide based on their varying global warming potentials. 
o The largest contributor to total emissions was fuel combustion in on-road vehicles.  
o 
The next two largest emissions sources were grid-purchased electricity and natural gas use 
in homes, buildings and facilities within the City. 
• 
Without any future actions (i.e., under “business-as-usual” conditions, also known as BAU 
forecast), GHG emissions are expected to increase through 2050. However, with an accounting 
of the impact of the City joining CEA and additional State, and Federal actions (i.e., under 
“legislative Adjusted Business-As-Usual” conditions, also known as ABAU forecast), emissions 
are expected to decline from 2018 levels through 2050. 
o 
The primary drivers of modeled emissions reductions from local, State, and Federal 
interventions are increased grid-supplied renewable electricity from joining CEA, 
implementation of the renewables portfolio standard through Senate Bill (SB) 100 and SB 
1020, as well as increased zero-emission vehicle penetration from Advanced Clean Cars II 
regulations. 


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
ES-4 
Greenhouse Gas Reduction Targets and Gap 
The GHG reduction targets of the CAP Update for the years 2030 and 2045 align with State's GHG 
emission reduction targets established in SB 32 and Assembly Bill (AB) 1279.1 The City’s GHG 
reduction target for the year 2050 aligns with the General Plan Update horizon year. These targets 
are updated from the City’s 2019 CAP to incorporate long-term GHG reduction targets for 2045 
and 2050, and to realign with the more recent 2018 GHG emissions inventory. The City’s GHG 
reduction targets in this CAP Update are: 
•
Reducing emissions 27 percent below 2018 levels by 2030 (aligned with SB 32),
•
Reducing emissions 90 percent below 2018 levels by 2045 (aligned with AB 1279), and
•
Maintaining emissions 90 percent below 2018 levels through 2050 (General Plan Update
horizon year).
These GHG reduction targets are not expected to be met without additional action beyond planned 
local, State, and Federal interventions. Table ES-1 and Figure ES-2 provide a summary of the ABAU 
forecast in comparison with the updated GHG reduction targets, and the emissions gap that would 
need to be reduced to meet the targets. The GHG reduction measures and actions included in this 
CAP Update were developed to address this emissions gap through local action.

Greenhouse Gas Reduction Measures and Actions 
The GHG reduction measures of the CAP Update 
primarily focus on increasing the accessibility and 
availability of renewable energy, promoting energy 
efficiency, 
providing 
alternative 
modes 
of 
transportation, supporting a transition to zero-
emission vehicles and equipment, improving water 
efficiency, 
and 
supporting 
increased 
carbon 
sequestration in the urban forest and natural lands. 
The total estimated reductions from all GHG reduction 
measures would be sufficient to meet the 2030 target; 
however, the 2045 and 2050 targets are not 
demonstrated to be met with the current measures 
alone. This CAP Update lays the foundation for the 
deep decarbonization needed to align with the State’s 
GHG reduction priorities. The City’s monitoring and 
reporting process for the CAP will provide the feedback loop to inform and refine longer-term GHG 
reduction initiatives as technology cost and availability evolves. 
 
Implementation and Monitoring  
The CAP Update sets monitoring procedures for measuring progress as GHG reduction measures 
and actions are implemented over time. City staff will annually evaluate the implementation status 
of each action by monitoring Key Performance Indicators and reporting progress towards the 
interim and final GHG goals in a CAP Annual Report. GHG emissions inventory updates will be 
Measure Highlights 
• 
Increase renewable energy availability to residents and businesses by providing 100% 
renewable/zero-carbon electricity through the Clean Energy Alliance (CEA) by 2030.  
• 
Increase solar photovoltaic (PV) capacity in the City through financial incentives and local 
building code updates.  
• 
Reduce emissions from organic solid waste by increasing Citywide landfill diversion rates.  
• 
Updating local building codes to encourage electrification of the City’s building stock and 
reducing natural gas use.  
• 
Support policies in the City’s General Plan Update and Smart & Sustainable Corridors 
Specific Plan to encourage infill development along existing transit corridors where active 
transportation and transit are most viable.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY  
ES-7 
completed biennially or more frequently as data become available. The CAP annual reports will 
identify emission reduction actions that need to be improved, adjusted, or replaced. Finally, the City 
will prepare a comprehensive update to the CAP every five years. 
The CAP Update has been prepared and adopted as a “qualified” CAP consistent with the California 
Environmental Quality Act (CEQA) guidelines. Under the provisions of a qualified CAP, if a 
development project in Oceanside aligns with the City's goals and reduces emissions consistent with 
the CAP Update, it can streamline the analysis of its GHG emissions under CEQA. Within one year of 
CAP adoption the City will develop and adopt guidelines for discretionary projects subject to CEQA 
to demonstrate consistency with the CAP during the standard development review process.

INTRODUCTION 
1 
1-1 
1 
Introduction 
The City of Oceanside (City) is committed to providing a high quality of life for the community while 
supporting sustainable land use patterns, healthy living, economic development, and community 
character. By using energy more efficiently, harnessing renewable energy to power buildings, 
reducing, reusing, recycling, and composting waste, conserving water, and enhancing access to 
sustainable transportation modes, the City can invest local revenue in its economy, create new 
green jobs, and improve residents’ quality of life in sustainable ways. To that end, the City has 
developed and implemented a Climate Action Plan (CAP) that integrates the City’s past and current 
greenhouse gas (GHG) reduction efforts with additional measures that seek to balance emissions 
reductions with other priorities, including quality of life, housing, employment, and fiscal 
responsibility.  
The City first adopted a CAP in 2019 through the first phase of its comprehensive General Plan 
Update (GPU). To inform this CAP Update, the City has assessed its progress in implementing GHG 
emissions reduction measures outlined in the 2019 CAP, subsequent regional policy initiatives 
related to climate mitigation, and new State legislation that establishes and refines the State’s 
expectations for local climate action. The City has sought to prepare an updated CAP that 1) 
leverages regional climate mitigation efforts, 2) aligns with State emissions reduction targets, and 
3) can be effectively implemented without extensive reallocation of City resources. 
1.1 
CLIMATE ACTION PLAN OVERVIEW 
A CAP is a planning document that outlines measures and 
actions a jurisdiction will implement to improve 
community resiliency to climate change through 
reduction of local GHG emissions. Development of a CAP 
is a data-based process in which jurisdictions evaluate the 
volume of GHGs emitted by activities within their 
boundary, such as energy use, waste generation, and 
vehicle travel, during a baseline year and set a future target for reducing GHG emissions through 
policies, programs, and incentives. The success of a CAP requires action at all levels of a community, 
where the role of a local government is to support community members in reducing the carbon 
footprint of daily activities. These governmental actions can take many forms, such as providing 
educational opportunities, adopting codes that support low-carbon buildings and transportation 
options, and decarbonizing essential services.  
Climate Action Plans serve as a 
policy framework for reducing GHG 
emissions at the local level while 
maximizing community benefit. 


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
1-2 
CAPs developed by local governments in California are typically aligned with California’s goals and 
strategies for reducing Statewide GHG emissions. Since 2006, the State has adopted near- and 
long-term GHG reduction targets through legislation that align with the best available climate 
science and global climate stabilization efforts. More recently, the Paris Agreement of 2015 set 
long-term goals for all nations to substantially reduce GHG emissions to hold global temperature 
increase to well below 2°C (35.6°F) above pre-industrial levels and pursue efforts to limit it to 1.5°C 
(34.7°F) above pre-industrial levels. In order to meet these climate stabilization goals, global net-
zero emissions would need to be achieved by 2050, in which all GHG emissions sources are 
balanced by carbon removal (United Nations ND). California established a trajectory for meeting 
this target when Assembly Bill (AB) 1279 was signed into law in 2022, setting a Statewide target for 
reaching net-zero emissions by 2045. Local jurisdictions 
are encouraged by the State to develop strategies for 
reducing their fair share of emissions in support of limiting 
the impacts of climate change in the world’s seventh 
largest economy.  
The City recognizes the importance of swift and 
concerted action to address the challenge of climate 
change, such that residents, workers, and visitors are 
protected from the worst impacts of this phenomenon. 
The CAP, and this subsequent update, is a key strategy 
document that strives to reduce the City’s contribution to 
climate change in a manner that supports a healthy, 
connected, and economically prosperous community.  
1.2 
2019 CLIMATE ACTION PLAN 
In April 2019, the City Council adopted Oceanside’s first CAP, which was prepared with Phase 1 of 
the General Plan Update (GPU). The 2019 CAP sought to align local emission reduction efforts with 
State legislation while balancing community interests and compliance with existing environmental 
regulations. It laid out the framework for how the City would address its fair share of GHG 
emissions while providing resources for residents, business owners, and others in the community 
to reduce their own emissions. 
The 2019 CAP’s emission reduction targets were developed to align with the adopted State 
legislation at the time, including AB 32, Senate Bill (SB) 32, and Executive Order S-03-05. 
Implementation of this CAP’s measures resulted in several milestones for the City in addressing 
climate change. These actions include: 
• 
Joining the Clean Energy Alliance (CEA) to deliver a higher share of renewably-sourced 
electricity to Oceanside customers;  
• 
Adopting Site Regulation Zoning Ordinances requiring certain types of new development to 
install renewable energy facilities; 
• 
Expediting permitting for solar photovoltaic installations;  
gO’side Electric Shuttle

1-3 
• 
Adopting the Zero Waste Plan 2020 Update; 
• 
Developing requirements around preferential parking and charging facilities for electric 
vehicles; 
• 
Adopting a Transport Demand Management ordinance for new commercial developments  
1.3 
2024 CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
This CAP Update builds upon the goals and successes of the 2019 CAP, is based on a more recent 
GHG emissions analysis, and reflects State legislation adopted since the City’s 2019 CAP was 
developed. The City has prepared the CAP Update as part of the second phase of a comprehensive 
GPU. Through this update, the City is upholding its commitment to enhance the quality of life in 
Oceanside to the highest standards through sustainable land use patterns, coastal resilience, 
resources that promote healthy living, and enhancement of the visual character of the community. 
The CAP Update outlines a variety of strategies and measures designed to reduce the anticipated 
GHG emissions from future development projected by the GPU and reduce local GHG emissions in 
accordance with State goals by addressing energy efficiency, renewable energy, low and zero-
emission transportation options, water conservation, solid waste management, and urban forestry. 
CAP Update Purpose and Objectives 
The CAP Update represents an important step in addressing global climate change and its effects 
on the City. The CAP Update intends to attain alignment with State legislation and directives and 
to continuously improve the City’s climate action efforts through fine-tuning emissions reduction 
measures by drawing from past achievements, leveraging Federal, State, and regional support, 
adaptive management, and adopting the latest best practices and new technology. The goals of 
the CAP encompass a comprehensive approach to addressing climate change including the 
following objectives: 
 
Electric Vehicle Charging on North Horne St.  
Residential Solar 


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
1-4 
• 
Aligning local efforts with State climate 
strategies; 
• 
Achieving climate stabilization;  
• 
Fostering economic stability;  
• 
Creating opportunities for employment in 
emerging economic sectors; 
• 
Realizing cost savings with a positive 
return on investment;  
• 
Promoting equity; and  
• 
Generating environmental co-benefits.  
With the implementation of the CAP Update, the City seeks to generate a range of environmental, 
economic, and public health co-benefits like improved public health through active transportation 
and clean energy initiatives resulting in cleaner air, the preservation of Oceanside’s valuable urban 
forest and improving the green jobs market. The CAP Update measures strive for farmland and 
habitat conservation by focusing future development in infill areas, resulting in positive outcomes 
like safeguarding essential ecosystems and promoting biodiversity. These efforts contribute to 
enhancing visual quality, making Oceanside more enjoyable for residents and more appealing to 
tourists. Through a multifaceted strategy, the CAP Update strives to create a sustainable and just 
future for all community members. 
Scope of the CAP Update 
The CAP Update focuses on efforts the City can take to reduce GHG emissions that result from 
activities that occur within its geographic boundary and over which the City has legal authority to 
influence. As such, some GHG emissions are excluded from the scope of the CAP Update as they 
cannot be directly controlled or influenced through local government actions. Some excluded GHG 
emissions sources include marine vessel fuel use, agriculture, and use of consumer products, as 
these emissions sources are outside of the City’s legal authority to regulate. However, the City 
recognizes that consumer choices have an impact on the environment and through this CAP Update 
will encourage residents and visitors to prioritize locally produced foods and products while 
supporting local small businesses.  
CAP Update Elements 
The CAP Update includes four key components that create a holistic strategy for the City to address 
climate change. Generally, these include a baseline Citywide GHG emissions analysis, setting GHG 
reduction targets aligned with State goals, developing GHG reduction measures and the associated 
actions for implementation, and procedures for monitoring and reporting progress on the 
implementation of GHG reduction measures. Figure 1-1 provides a summary of the elements and 
process for development of the CAP Update.  
Garrison Creek Trail  


 
INTRODUCTION 
1 
1-5 
 
Figure 1-1 
Components of the Climate Action Plan Development Process 
Greenhouse Gas Emissions Analysis 
The initial evaluation of GHG emissions is called a GHG emissions inventory, which establishes an 
understanding of how GHG emissions are generated within a physical boundary such that policies 
can be developed to address individual GHG emissions sources. After a GHG inventory is 
completed, a forecast of future GHG emissions is developed which provides insight into how GHG 
emissions may change with growth and how external factors, such as State and Federal legislation, 
may influence how GHG emissions evolve. As part of the CAP Update, the City completed a 2018 
communitywide GHG emissions inventory to establish a baseline for the GHG emissions forecast 
and setting associated reduction targets. The GHG emissions forecast evaluates how GHG 
emissions may change in the future under two scenarios. The first scenario is a business-as-usual 
scenario, which looks at how growth alone would influence future GHG emissions. The second 
scenario is a legislative adjusted business-as-usual scenario, which provides an understanding of 
how currently adopted State and Federal legislation may affect future GHG emissions, as well as 
the impact of the City joining CEA. Clean Energy Alliance (CEA) is a community choice energy (CCE) 
provider that purchases electricity from a portfolio of renewable and carbon-free sources to meet 
the community’s electricity needs, offering an alternative to San Diego Gas & Electric. This analysis 
sets the stage for areas where GHG reduction efforts by the City should be focused to synchronize 
with State and Federal regulations.  
Greenhouse Gas Reduction Targets 
Another key component of a CAP is setting GHG reduction targets that guide how aggressive GHG 
reduction policies should be. The GHG reduction targets of the CAP Update align with Statewide 
legislative adopted targets, including Senate Bill (SB) 32 and Assembly Bill (AB) 1279. The CAP 
Update has developed new GHG reduction targets from those adopted with the 2019 CAP, such 
that the targets can be evaluated from the new 2018 baseline GHG emissions inventory and follow 
the most recent State guidance. This results in two GHG reduction targets for the years 2030 and 
2045, which are evaluated based on a reduction below 2018 communitywide GHG emissions levels. 
An additional long-term target for the year 2050 is included in the CAP Update, which aligns with 
the General Plan Update horizon year. The GHG reduction targets included in the CAP Update are: 


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
1-6 
• 
Reducing emissions 27 percent below 2018 levels by 2030 (aligned with SB 32),  
• 
Reducing emissions 90 percent below 2018 levels by 2045 (aligned with AB 1279), and 
• 
Maintaining emissions 90 percent below 2018 levels through 2050 (General Plan Update 
horizon year). 
Greenhouse Gas Reduction Measures and Actions 
The CAP Update includes a suite of policies focused on reducing local GHG emissions, which are 
called GHG reduction measures. Each GHG reduction measure is focused on reducing emissions 
from specific sources under the City’s jurisdictional authority, such as on-road transportation and 
land use within its borders. Measures are implemented through a discrete set of actions.  
The core GHG reduction measures focus on increasing 
the delivery of renewable energy, enhancing building 
energy efficiency, providing alternative modes of 
transportation, supporting a transition to zero-
emission vehicles and equipment, improving water use 
efficiency, 
and 
supporting 
increased 
carbon 
sequestration in the urban forest, natural lands, and 
working lands. In total, the CAP Update includes 17 
GHG reduction measures with 104 implementation 
actions to achieve the GHG reduction targets.  
Implementation Monitoring and Reporting 
Understanding the effectiveness of GHG reduction measures and enabling flexibility to make data-
informed decisions about if and when measures should be modified is key to achieving long-term 
GHG reduction targets. The CAP Update sets monitoring procedures for measuring progress as 
GHG reduction measures and actions are implemented over time. To appropriately allocate 
resources to programs and policies, the City must execute actions on their intended timeline and 
determine how effective they are at achieving the intended results. Additionally, the CAP Update 
includes procedures for reporting implementation progress to the public and decision-makers such 
that the City can demonstrate fulfillment of the commitments made in the CAP Update.  
CAP Update Process 
In early 2020, the City began the simultaneous process of completing its GPU and the creation of a 
Smart and Sustainable Corridors Specific Plan. In early 2023, the City kicked off the CAP Update as 
a companion to these efforts, in which the CAP Update is closely tied to future buildout assumptions 
and policy initiatives. The City completed the CAP Update’s GHG emissions analysis in fall of 2023, 
which included preparation a GHG emissions inventory and forecast, and developing GHG 
Metrolink Commuter Train

reduction targets. From fall of 2023 to early 2024, 
the City developed and refined GHG emissions 
reduction measures and actions to be included in

other stakeholders was conducted throughout the 
entirety of the project.  
As part of the General Plan Update and CAP Update process, the City engaged with the community 
using various methods to understand priorities and needs. The CAP Update is closely tied to the 
GPU and its environmental documentation as a CEQA-qualified CAP (see Sections 2.7 and 5.3). 
Many of the GPU’s core elements are included in the CAP Update. As such, the extensive 
community engagement performed for the various GPU components was leveraged to incorporate 
community priorities into the CAP Update. Outreach conducted to solicit community values and 
priorities included numerous workshops and open houses, in both virtual and in-person formats, 
surveys, and ad hoc discussions with small groups and individual community members.  
In October 2023, the City issued an online survey specific to the CAP Update in both English and 
Spanish. The survey was focused on both educating community members on the CAP Update 
process, while soliciting feedback on the types of measures that would receive high participation. 
Nearly 470 community members participated in the survey. The responses to the survey were used 
in the development of the GHG reduction measures, which highlighted the importance of education 
and information sharing, ensuring requirements are cost-effective for businesses and residents, and 
considerations for potential negative secondary impacts to low-income residents and historically 
disadvantaged communities. The results of the survey are provided for reference in Appendix C. 
CAP Update Organization 
Chapter 2 provides background information describing the science underlying climate change and 
the impacts anticipated for the City. It also describes existing Federal and State regulations related 
to GHG emissions and climate change, regional coordination including transportation planning, and 
other regional planning efforts related to the CAP. Chapter 3 includes details on the City’s baseline 
emissions inventory and projections and establishes reduction targets for 2030, 2045, and 2050. 
Chapters 4 provides detail on the GHG reduction measures and associated implementation actions 
of the CAP Update, including summaries for the intent and background of each measure. Lastly, 
Chapter 5 outlines the implementation and monitoring procedures, including detail on how 
progress will be measured, implementation timelines and responsibilities, future updates to the 
CAP and its relationship to CEQA and the GPU.

Background 
The City of Oceanside (City) is committed to providing residents, workers and visitors with a 
healthy and resilient environment that fosters economic opportunity and prosperity. These goals 
align with climate mitigation efforts central to sustainability themes in the General Plan Update 
(GPU). The City acknowledges the threat of human-induced climate change and recognizes the 
community benefits that can be achieved by reducing the City’s contribution to this phenomenon. 
For example, improving energy efficiency reduces ratepayer costs and, in turn, socioeconomic 
costs for community members while reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from energy 
generation. Enhancing neighborhood connectivity and walkability fosters livable and healthy 
communities while reducing vehicle travel. Restoring watershed health and connectivity can 
increase carbon sequestration while improving sediment flow, supporting coastal resilience 
through living shoreline and coastal dune formation. These are only some examples of 
opportunities the City is capitalizing on as part of City programs and long-range planning through 
the GPU.  
This chapter provides the background context for development of the Climate Action Plan (CAP) 
Update. It includes an introduction to the science behind climate change and its current and 
anticipated impacts on the City. Next is a discussion of the drivers of climate action and initiatives 
to address climate change at the Federal, State and regional levels, including a summary of actions 
the City has taken that reduce its contribution to climate change. This chapter also discusses the 
intersection between the CAP Update, the General Plan Update and the California Environmental 
Quality Act (CEQA), as well as future efforts such as Local Coastal Program (LCP) updates and the 
development of long-term planning and climate adaptation policies. 
2.1 
CLIMATE SCIENCE 
The physical phenomena behind climate change contribute to the greenhouse effect, a natural 
process that insulates the Earth and helps regulate its temperature through the presence of GHGs 
in the atmosphere. The greenhouse effect is demonstrated in Figure 2-1. Metaphorically, GHGs, 
such as water vapor, carbon dioxide (CO2), methane (CH4), and nitrous oxide (N2O), act as a blanket 
surrounding our planet. This “blanket” is necessary to sustain life on Earth—without it, 
temperatures would be much colder, leaving Earth unsuitable for human life. However, even 
though the greenhouse effect is a necessary, natural process, it has been exacerbated by human 
activities, notably through the burning of fossil fuels and the removal of natural “sinks,” such as 
forests and grasslands, that serve to trap some of the GHGs that would otherwise reach the 
atmosphere. These human activities have released, and are continuing to release, an excess 
amount of GHGs into the atmosphere, causing the Earth’s climate to warm at an unprecedented 
rate. 


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
2-2 
 
Source: Ascent Environmental 2021. 
Figure 2-1 
The Greenhouse Effect 
The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) published the Sixth Assessment Report 
(2021-2023), a science-based summary of the most recent research on the causes and impacts of 
climate change. According to the report, there is scientific consensus that observed increases in 
atmospheric GHG concentrations and the consequential warming of Earth’s atmosphere, oceans, 
and lands have “unequivocally” been caused by human activities and influence (IPCC 2021). The 
report further states that human activities are estimated to have caused over two (2) degrees 
Fahrenheit (°F) of warming across the globe compared to pre-industrial era levels (i.e., before the 
year 1900), and the global average temperature is expected to increase by up to approximately 
8 °F by the end of the century unless additional efforts to reduce GHG emissions are made.  
As California continues to experience rising temperatures, more extreme storms, more frequent 
and intense wildfires, and intense droughts, it has become evident that the effects of climate 
change are already occurring. The impacts of climate change in California vary across the state due 
to its diverse biophysical setting, climate, and community characteristics, but even modest changes 
in temperature and precipitation patterns could have significant effects on the state’s ecosystems. 
At a regional level, the average annual temperature in the San Diego region is projected to increase 
between 5 and 10 ˚F by the end of the 21st century (University of San Diego 2023).  Driven by 


 
BACKGROUND 
2 
2-3 
rising global temperatures, ocean thermal expansion, and melting ice, sea levels along Southern 
California’s coast could increase by as much as one foot by 2050 and at least three feet by 2100 
(City of Oceanside 2018). Regional variation in these projections is expected. These changes are 
expected to result in adverse impacts on transportation infrastructure, energy systems, human 
health and safety, and economic prosperity in the region (OPR, CEC, and CNRA 2018).  
2.2 
LOCAL CLIMATE CHANGE IMPACTS 
As a coastal community with a substantial wildland-urban interface (WUI), floodplains, sloping 
terrain, farmland, and a variety of microclimates, Oceanside is exposed to a wide range of natural 
hazards that threaten public safety and the built environment. Wildfire has affected Oceanside, 
with the 2017 Lilac Fire being the most significant wildfire in recent history. The City’s highly 
eroded coastline is susceptible to wave uprush and inundation during king tides and high surf 
events, which has damaged many beachfront homes and public facilities in recent years. Properties 
adjacent to Loma Alta Creek experience flooding during major rain events, as do segments of State 
Route 78 in proximity to Buena Vista Creek. The segment of Coast Highway that crosses Buena 
Vista Lagoon can become impassable 
during major rain events. Droughts have 
threatened agricultural operations in the 
South Morro Hills area, where farmers 
struggle with the high cost of water. 
Landslides have damaged and destroyed 
single-family homes in Rancho del Oro and 
other neighborhoods. In short, much of 
Oceanside is vulnerable to natural hazards, 
with the City’s coastline, northern and 
eastern boundaries, hillsides, and areas 
near Loma Alta Creek and Buena Vista 
Creek and Lagoon being particularly at risk.  
Oceanside’s proximity to the Pacific Ocean coastline is core to the City’s identity. However, this 
characteristic presents additional challenges that will be exacerbated by climate change and 
associated sea level rise (SLR). In Oceanside, SLR is expected to accelerate as climate change 
continues. Projections indicate that local ocean levels are expected to rise an additional eight 
inches to one and a half feet by 2045, and more than three feet by 2100 (City of Oceanside 2018). 
The range of SLR impacts that will be realized are driven by the ability to reduce global GHG 
emissions. 
The City’s efforts to reduce local GHG emissions provide a direct benefit to protecting coastal 
access, property, and critical infrastructure from the impacts of SLR. 
In addition to increased vulnerability to natural hazards, Oceanside’s coastal zone faces new 
challenges, including increasing visitor traffic, more stringent water quality and habitat 
management requirements and aging infrastructure. In the past, natural hazards have caused 
significant beach erosion and damage to infrastructure and property. In the future, these 
Flooding on Highway 78 in Oceanside on January 
23, 2024 (NBC San Diego) 


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
2-4 
vulnerabilities exacerbated by climate change will potentially increase as the intensity and 
frequency of hazardous events increase with climate change. In response, the City is currently 
developing its Coastal Hazard Adaptation Plan and updating its Local Coastal Program and land 
uses through the General Plan Update. The City has developed this CAP Update to reduce its 
contribution to climate change and establish a long-range vision for the community. 
2.3 
STATE AND FEDERAL REGULATORY FRAMEWORK 
In response to the steady increases in GHG 
emissions and the threat of global climate 
change, Federal and State governments have 
already taken several steps to reduce GHG 
emissions. These efforts, and the legislative 
background summarized in the following 
sections, provide important policy direction 
and context for this CAP Update. 
Federal Climate Legislation 
The Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act 
The Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act (IIJA), also known as the Bipartisan Infrastructure Act, 
was passed by Congress on November 6, 2021 to provide a once-in-generation bipartisan 
infrastructure bill. IIJA is the largest long-term investment to rebuild roads, bridges, and railways; 
expand access to high-speed internet; tackle the climate crisis; advance environmental justice; and 
invest in underrepresented communities. By investing in transportation and public transit, GHG 
emissions will be reduced and clean, zero-emission vehicles will be incentivized.  
Corporate Average Fuel Economy Standards 
On April 1, 2022, the U.S. Department of Transportation updated its Corporate Average Fuel 
Economy (CAFE) standards to apply to mileage standards for passenger cars and light trucks. The 
standards apply to model years 2024 and 2026, requiring an industry-wide fuel economy average 
of approximately 49 miles per gallon for passenger cars and light trucks for model year 2026. The 
new standards will increase fuel efficiency eight (8) percent annually for model years 2024 to 2025, 
and 10 percent for model year 2026. 
Inflation Reduction Act 
The Inflation Reduction Act (IRA) was signed into law on August 16, 2022. It was intended to 
address climate pollution by investing in GHG reduction strategies. The IRA built on the 
opportunities passed into law in IIJA by supporting projects across electric vehicle charging, 
renewable power infrastructure and climate resilience. Examples include supporting U.S. 
manufacturing of solar panels, wind turbines, batteries, and critical materials by expanding 
production tax credits; providing direct incentives for American families to decarbonize their 
homes; and establishing the Greenhouse Gas Reduction Fund (GGRF) to support rapid deployment 
California’s current legislative GHG Reduction 
Targets include: 
• Reducing GHG emissions to 40% below 
1990 levels by 2030 
• Reducing GHG emissions to 85% below 
1990 levels by 2045, and achieving net zero 
emissions through carbon dioxide removal 


 
BACKGROUND 
2 
2-5 
of low- to zero-emission technologies to benefit disadvantaged communities. In 2025, the One 
Big Beautiful Bill rolled back some provisions of the IRA, such as reducing the applicability of the 
Commercial Solar Tax Credit and elimination of residential energy efficiency and electric vehicle 
charger incentives. Simlarly, the 2025 Working Families Tax Cut eliminated funding for the GGRF.  
Renewable Fuel Standard Program 
The Renewable Fuel Standard (RFS) program was created under the Energy Policy Act of 2005 
and further expanded by the Energy Independence and Security Act of 2007, which amended the 
Clean Air Act (CAA). On June 3, 2022, the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) finalized a 
package of regulatory changes to set biofuel volumes for 2020, 2021, and 2022 to reach the 
objectives of the RFS. The goals are to increase the amounts of renewable fuels to be blended into 
transportation fuel in increasing amounts each year. The renewable fuels in the RFS program must 
emit lower levels of GHGs relative to the petroleum fuel it replaces.  
California Climate Change Legislation 
California has established itself as a leader in addressing climate change through an evolving suite 
of legislation to set statewide GHG reduction targets and develop strategies to meet them. 
California was one of the first states to set legislative statewide GHG reduction targets in 2006, 
and again one of the first to set a long-term net zero target. The following provides a summary of 
the key legislative actions the State has taken to address climate change, with an overview 
presented in Figure 2-2. 
Executive Order (EO) S-3-05 was signed in 2005, which directed California to reduce statewide 
GHG emissions to 1990 levels by 2020 and to 80 percent below 1990 levels by 2050. A year later, 
the Global Warming Solutions Act of 2006 (Assembly Bill [AB] 32) was passed, establishing 
regulatory, reporting, and market mechanisms to achieve quantifiable reductions in GHG 
emissions. AB 32 put a cap on GHGs and set a target of reducing statewide emissions to 1990 
levels by 2020. In 2008, the California Air Resources Board (CARB) developed the Climate Change 
Scoping Plan: A Framework for Change (2008 Scoping Plan) as part of its role in implementing AB 
32 and EO S-3-05. The 2008 Scoping Plan, along with its update in 2014, described the approach 
California would take to reduce GHG emissions to achieve reduction targets and goals. In 2016, 
California achieved the AB 32 target of reducing GHG emissions to 1990 levels, 4 years ahead of 
schedule, based on its reported statewide 2016 inventory (CARB 2020). The annual statewide 
GHG inventories for 2017-2020 have shown that the State continues to achieve the AB 32 target. 
In September 2016, Senate Bill (SB) 32 codified into statute the midterm 2030 target of 40 percent 
below 1990 levels by 2030. In November 2017, CARB published California’s 2017 Climate Change 
Scoping Plan Update (2017 Scoping Plan) (CARB 2017), which laid out the framework for achieving 
the 2030 target established in SB 32. The 2017 Scoping Plan identifies GHG reductions by 
emissions sector to achieve a statewide emissions level that is 40 percent below 1990 levels by 
2030.  


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
2-6 
The California Climate Crisis Act (AB 1279), signed in September 2022, further established the 
State’s GHG emissions reduction targets of an 85 percent reduction in anthropogenic emissions 
below 1990 levels by 2045 and net zero GHG emissions by 2045.  
The Sea Level Rise Planning and Adaptation Act (SB 272) in California requires local governments 
to develop sea-level rise plans and adaptation strategies. These plans must be updated regularly 
based on sea-level rise projections and conditions. The act aims to strengthen coastal resilience 
and protect communities and infrastructure from the impacts of sea-level rise exacerbated by 
climate change. The bill requires local governments that receive approval for a plan, by the deadline 
of 2034, to be prioritized for funding for the implementation of adaptation strategies and 
recommended projects in their approved sea level rise plan.  
2022 Climate Change Scoping Plan 
CARB’s 2022 Scoping Plan for Achieving Carbon Neutrality (2022 Scoping Plan) assesses progress 
toward the statutory 2030 target, while laying out a path to achieving carbon neutrality no later 
than 2045. The 2022 Scoping Plan focuses on outcomes needed to achieve carbon neutrality by 
assessing paths for clean technology, energy deployment, natural and working lands, and others, 
and is designed to meet the State’s long-term climate objectives and support a range of economic, 
environmental, energy security, environmental justice, and public health priorities.  
While the 2022 Scoping Plan provides a framework for the State to meet its long-term GHG 
reduction targets, it also provides guidance for local jurisdictions on how their climate planning 
efforts can align with State priorities. The 2022 Scoping Plan provides an overview of the feasible 
and cost-effective pathways for climate mitigation, including key focus areas of electrifying most 
commercial and residential buildings, reducing vehicle travel with smart-growth infill development, 
preserving natural and working lands, achieving 100 percent renewable electricity, and achieving 
high rates of zero-emission vehicle adoption. Local jurisdictions are encouraged by CARB to align 
with these priorities in their climate planning efforts to support the State in meeting its aggressive 
GHG reduction targets.

Major Climate Legislation in the State of California 
2.4 ADDRESSING CLIMATE CHANGE THROUGH REGIONAL COLLABORATION  
Regional action and collaboration are needed to effectively address the climate emergency, and 
various programs have been developed in the San Diego region to help expand local capacity and 
synchronize efforts in climate change mitigation. The ability of individual jurisdictions to 


 
BACKGROUND 
2 
2-7 
collaborate and coordinate efforts also presents opportunities for inter-jurisdictional coordination, 
leveraging economies of scale that can help reduce costs for program implementation, and 
positioning the region for State and Federal funding opportunities. The following provides a 
selection of regional programs and initiatives in which the City engages to support regional and 
local climate change mitigation.  
SANDAG Regional Climate Planning framework 
The San Diego Association of Governments (SANDAG) is the region’s transportation planning 
agency which leads the Regional Climate Action Planning Framework (ReCAP). ReCAP identifies 
best practices and guidance for preparing CAPs for the San Diego region and assists in monitoring 
implementation of local CAPs. As part of the framework, jurisdictions were provided with biennial 
GHG emissions inventory updates and implementation tracking data. This effort provided essential 
support for jurisdictions to plan, monitor and update their adopted CAPs. As of 2025, ReCAP faces 
an uncertain future to continue providing these service to local cities based on funding limitations.  
San Diego County’s Regional Decarbonization Framework 
San Diego County's science‐based Regional Decarbonization Framework promotes public and 
private interests working together to move the entire San Diego region toward zero carbon 
emissions in transportation, buildings, energy, and land use by mid‐century. The Regional 
Decarbonization Framework will help policymakers across San Diego identify ways their cities and 
the unincorporated areas of the county can decarbonize while working in harmony with 
neighboring jurisdictions as well as community, industry, environmental, business, and labor 
groups.  
2.5 
CLIMATE MITIGATION EFFORTS IN OCEANSIDE 
The City of Oceanside has a number of current plans and programs that enhance energy 
conservation, water efficiency, waste reduction, and active transportation. Implementing these 
programs and projects allows the City to protect and enhance natural resources, reduce the City's 
carbon footprint and save money for Oceanside ratepayers. Below is a short list of programs and 
related projects that the City is undertaking that support climate mitigation efforts. 
Green Oceanside Program 
The Green Oceanside campaign was established to share resources and educate residents, 
businesses and visitors about how to engage in more sustainable everyday practices. Through 
collaboration and partnerships between City departments, local businesses, community groups, 
schools and residents, the Green Oceanside campaign provides an avenue for the public to learn 
how to actively protect the environment through community involvement and engagement by 
offering opportunities for people to participate in events and programs that provide a direct 
benefit to the local environment while reducing the City’s carbon footprint.  


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
2-8 
Zero Waste Program 
The City’s Zero Waste Program provides resources to 
community members on ways to increase diversion of 
waste from landfills and information about compliance 
with the requirements of SB 1383. As part of the 
program, the City developed a citywide Zero Waste 
Plan, first adopted in 2012 and updated in 2020, to 
outline a strategy to achieve a 90 percent citywide 
waste diversion target. Current efforts are focused on 
encouraging proper recycling of eligible materials, 
diversion of organic waste from landfills which 
contribute to GHG emissions and edible food recovery, 
consistent with regulatory requirements under SB 
1383. 
Watershed Protection Program 
The goal of the Oceanside Watershed Protection Program is to improve water quality in local creeks, 
rivers, and the ocean, and comply with State and Federal environmental regulations. This multi-
faceted program is designed to achieve its goal through compliance with the San Diego Regional 
Municipal Stormwater Permit issued by the Regional Water Quality Control Board (RWQCB) and by 
educating the community about pollution prevention practices. The Buena Vista Creek Restoration 
Planning Project is one of multiple projects under the program that will help improve existing riparian 
habitat, enhance water quality, and provide 
recreational and educational opportunities for 
the surrounding community. Efforts under this 
program to restore and enhance watersheds 
provides increased opportunity for carbon 
sequestration within Oceanside consistent 
with policies under the General Plan Update. 
Furthermore, these projects will improve 
hydrologic 
connectivity 
and 
sediment 
transport to the coast and sediment transport 
to support resiliency against coastal storms 
and rising seas. 
Water Efficiency Program 
The City’s Water Conservation Master Plan and Urban Water Management Plan (2020) are guiding 
documents for Green Oceanside’s water conservation program. The City also implements 
mandatory water use efficiency measures through its ordinances to ensure adequate water 
supplies even in drought conditions. Improving water efficiency reduces the energy required to 
supply and treat water, thereby reducing GHG emissions. The 2020 Urban Water Master Plan, is 
expected to be updated and adopted by late 2026.

Pure Water Oceanside Program 
Pure Water Oceanside aims to purify recycled 
water to create a new, local source of high-quality 
drinking water that is clean, safe, drought-proof 
and environmentally sound. The program will 
provide more than 30% of the City's water supply 
through 
indirect 
potable 
reuse 
technology, 
allowing for reduced reliance on imported water 
which requires much more energy to deliver to 
Oceanside than local water supplies.  
Renewable Energy Program 
The goal of the program is to provide a secure 
energy source alternative that is below the cost 
spent for the same amount of energy purchased 
from San Diego Gas and Electric (SDG&E). As an 
example, the San Luis Rey Solar Panel Project, 
produces an alternative source of energy for the 
San Luis Rey Water Reclamation Facility. Future 
solar projects at City facilities will occur through 
the Opterra energy services contract. 
In 2022, the City officially joined Clean Energy 
Alliance (CEA), a community choice aggregation 
program, which provides an alternative power 
source to SDG&E to provide a more cost-
competitive choice of renewable energy. CEA 
began delivering electricity to over 74,000 Oceanside accounts comprised of 50 percent 
renewable sources in April 2024, with a goal to increase to 100 percent renewably sourced power 
by 2035.  
Urban Forestry Program 
The Urban Forestry Program manages approximately 48,000 trees in the City’s rights-of-way, 
facilities, and parks. With over 200 different species, varieties, and cultivators, the City’s street 
tree population is diverse and provides significant benefits for residents and visitors. The Urban 
Forestry Program is responsible for a significant portion of the urban forests in the City. 
Green Oceanside Business Network 
The Green Oceanside Business Network is a program designed by the City to spur innovation and 
environmental responsibility while helping Oceanside businesses prosper in the green economy. 
The program provides opportunities for local businesses to showcase and share practices that 
reduce waste and energy use, and increase water and energy efficiency. While the program is not

Pedestrian Master Plan 
The City of Oceanside 2009 Pedestrian Master Plan identified opportunities for improvements to 
the walking environment throughout Oceanside to support community members in being able to 
safely walk to destinations. Improving the walking conditions in the City supports residents, 
workers, and visitors in opting to walk to destinations instead of driving. Both the Bicycle and 
Pedestrian Master Plans will be simultaneously updated into the new Active Transportation Plan 
that will be developed following adoption of General Plan Update’s second phase.

gO’Side Shuttle Program 
The City, in partnership with SANDAG and Visit Oceanside, operates low-fare fully electric shuttle 
rides in parts of Oceanside. The gO’side shuttle pilot project aims to provide connections to the 
Oceanside Transit Station and relieve traffic congestion and parking challenges in Downtown 
Oceanside. It also makes it easier for people to navigate the area without relying on a car.  
The shuttle provides an accessible, zero-emissions option for people who want to access 
Oceanside’s shops and recreational activities, as well as dining, transit, healthcare, jobs and 
residential areas. The project is currently in its second year of operation and is a prime example of 
addressing “first mile – last mile” challenges in public transit. 
City Facilities Energy Improvements 
The City, in collaboration with Opterra Energy Services, is working on a transformative energy 
initiative aimed at enhancing sustainability, reducing costs and fostering community engagement. 
This initiative is projected to generate more than $26,000,000 in net savings for the City over the 
next 30 years. It includes significant energy efficiency gains through a series of measures at City 
facilities, including integrating 1.6 megawatts of solar alongside a 250-kilowatt energy storage 
system, replacing and refurbishing HVAC units; implementing a battery energy storage system and 
an energy management system; installing new, efficient distribution transformers and generator 
heat pumps; and upgrading interior and exterior lighting to LEDs. 
2.6 
COASTAL ADAPTATION EFFORTS IN OCEANSIDE 
With nearly four miles of coastline, the beach is a way of life in the City of Oceanside. The City’s 
economy depends on sustainable management of its coastline for fisheries, tourism, recreation, 
and more. The City’s Coastal Zone Management Program pursues grants and partnerships to adapt 
its coastline into a thriving resource for future generations. This includes updating the City’s Local 
Coastal Program to include a sea-level rise risk assessment and adoption of a Coastal Hazards 
Adaptation Plan. The Coastal Hazards Adaptation Plan reflects awareness of climate-induced sea 
level rise, emergency preparedness measures and coastline adaptation goals that will be achieved 
through specific, measurable initiatives. Examples of these projects include: 
RE:BEACH Oceanside Pilot Project 
This flagship initiative employs nature-based solutions to combat coastal erosion. The pilot project 
involves constructing two artificial headlands and an offshore reef to retain sand along the 
shoreline. In April 2025, the California Coastal Commission approved a $1.835 million grant to 
support baseline monitoring for this project, the results of which will inform future climate 
adaptation planning efforts.  


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
2-12
Sand Compatibility and Opportunistic Use Program (SCOUP) 
SCOUP aims to streamline beach nourishment by 
utilizing compatible sand from various sources, 
such as construction sites or dredging operations, 
to replenish eroded beaches with sand that would 
otherwise be disposed in a landfill. 
Coastal Dune Restoration Pilot Project 
This pilot project focuses on restoring coastal dune 
habitats at Harbor Beach, the San Luis Rey river 
mouth 
and 
North 
Strand 
beaches. 
Future 
restoration will enhance natural buffers against 
storm surge and sea level rise while supporting 
biodiversity. 
CoastSnap 
CoastSnap is a citizen science program that invites the public to contribute photographs of the 
coastline. These images help monitor beach changes over time, aiding in understanding erosion 
patterns and informing management decisions. 
SANDAG Regional Projects 
In collaboration with the SANDAG, Oceanside participates in regional efforts like the Regional 
Beach Sand Project III, which involves dredging and placing sand to replenish beaches, restore 
beach habitat and protect against erosion.  
2.7 
RELATIONSHIP TO THE GENERAL PLAN UPDATE 
In 2019, the City Council adopted Phase 1 of the 
General Plan Update and adopted two new 
General Plan elements addressing economic 
development and climate action. The Economic 
Development Element (EDE) informs future 
actions affecting the City’s fiscal resources and 
the local economy, centered around providing a high quality of life with economic prosperity, 
resilience, and collaboration. The Energy and Climate Action Element (ECAE) outlines goals and 
policies around the themes of energy efficiency/renewable energy, smart growth and multimodal 
transportation, zero waste, water conservation, local agriculture, urban greening, and sustainable 
consumption. These two new elements were accompanied by the City’s first CAP, which included 
both mandatory and voluntary emissions reduction measures to address citywide GHG emissions 
sources and set a pathway towards reaching GHG reduction targets aligned with State goals. The 
EDE, ECAE, and CAP constituted Phase 1 of the comprehensive General Plan Update and provided 
important guidance for the second phase of the General Plan Update, which involves updates to 
the City’s existing General Plan elements and a specific plan to guide growth in the City. 
The South Strand 
The CAP Update is being completed as part 
of Phase 2 of the City’s comprehensive 
General Plan Update.

Components of Phase 2 of the General Plan Update that are pertinent to this CAP Update are 
discussed below.  
Efficient and Compatible Land Use Element 
The City’s update to its Land Use Element, the Efficient and Compatible Land Use (ECLU) Element, 
determines how future growth will occur in Oceanside. The goals of the ECLU ensure compatibility 
between different land uses, accommodate demand for both housing and nonresidential uses, 
foster complete neighborhoods, and encourage efficient land use that creates synergies between 
different uses, enhances walkability and transit access, and reduces development pressure on 
outlying areas. The CAP Update has been developed to be consistent with the development 
patterns of the ECLU.  
Smart and Sustainable Corridors Specific Plan 
The City’s Smart and Sustainable Corridors Specific Plan (SSCSP) outlines strategies for promoting 
infill and redevelopment along the Mission Avenue, Oceanside Boulevard, and Vista Way corridors 
in the City. The SSCSP is a regulatory document that establishes land use and development 
standards for properties within the Specific Plan planning area. Prepared concurrently with the 
City’s updated General Plan, the SSCSP will implement General Plan goals and policies that 
promote efficient land use, a stronger sense of place, a range of mobility options, open space and 
farmland preservation, social engagement, and enhanced visual quality. 
2.8 
CALIFORNIA ENVIRONMENTAL QUALITY ACT 
CEQA is a State statute that requires public agencies to identify and publicly disclose the 
significant environmental impacts of new development and other projects, including GHG 
emissions impacts, and to avoid or mitigate those impacts if it is feasible to do so. CEQA, in its 
implementing guidelines, includes provisions to streamline the environmental review process of 
projects that are consistent with a “plan for the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions” that meets 
specified criteria, which are outlined in CEQA Guidelines Section 15183.5(b)(1). This CAP Update 
has been developed to be used as a streamlining tool for GHG emissions.  
Under the CEQA Guidelines, a project that is consistent with the City’s “qualified” CAP 2 is eligible 
for streamlined analysis of its GHG emissions impacts under CEQA. While project consistency with 
the CAP Update would ultimately need to be determined on a case-by-case basis, a consistent 
project would generally be one that is both consistent with the growth projections used to prepare 
the CAP Update and demonstrates alignment with the GHG reduction targets of the CAP Update. 
Alignment with the GHG reduction targets of the CAP Update may be determined through various 
pathways – a CAP consistency checklist that documents incorporation of all applicable GHG 
reduction measures into a project’s analysis, quantitative GHG thresholds, or some combination 
2 Under the CEQA Guidelines (15183.5 – Tiering and Streamlining the Analysis of Greenhouse Gas Emissions) this CAP meets the 
requirements of the California Code of Regulations as a “Plan for the Reduction of Greenhouse Gas Emissions” that may be used in 
a cumulative impacts analysis pursuant to CEQA Guidelines 15064(h)(3) and 15130(d).  


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
2-14 
thereof. The incremental GHG emissions from projects found to be consistent with the CAP 
Update may be determined to not have a cumulative considerable impact under CEQA. Projects 
eligible to utilize these streamlining provisions will be able to tier from and/or incorporate by 
reference the CAP Update’s programmatic review of GHG emissions in their CEQA analysis of 
cumulative GHG emissions. Within one year of CAP Update adoption the City will develop and 
adopt guidelines for discretionary projects subject to CEQA. The guidelines will provide one or 
more pathways to demonstrate consistency with the CAP Update during the standard 
development review process.

3-1 
3 
Greenhouse Gas Emissions Inventory, 
Projections, and Targets 
3.1 
OVERVIEW 
This chapter summarizes an accounting of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions generated by activities 
within the City, which is used to inform the development of GHG reduction measures for the 
Climate Action Plan (CAP) Update. This accounting presents the City of Oceanside’s (City’s) 
baseline GHG emissions levels in 2018 and the anticipated increase in emissions if the City takes no 
local actions through the year 2045. Additionally, this chapter establishes targets for reducing GHG 
emissions consistent with Statewide GHG reduction targets and goals for the years 2030, 2045, and 
2050. The analyses presented here are updates from those included in the City’s 2019 CAP and 
provide an updated set of reference points for the development of GHG reduction measures to 
meet GHG reduction targets aligned with current State legislation.  
3.2 
GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS INVENTORY  
Greenhouse Gas Inventory Background 
The GHG emissions inventory provides the existing conditions for the CAP Update, identifying the 
contribution of different communitywide GHG emissions sources in Oceanside and establishing a 
baseline for forecasting future emissions. The inventory has informed the GHG reduction targets and 
is essential to effective monitoring of progress towards reaching established targets over time. The 
GHG emissions inventory presented here is for the calendar year 2018, which was provided by the 
San Diego Association of Governments (SANDAG) through the Regional Climate Action Planning 
Framework (ReCAP) Snapshot program (SANDAG 2023)1. An updated GHG emissions inventory is 
used to provide a baseline for forecasting future emissions from the most recently available data, and 
a consistent baseline for future emissions inventories. While previous GHG inventories have been 
completed for Oceanside, including a 2013 inventory for the City’s 2019 CAP, the use of the updated 
baseline for 2018 allows for consistent comparison of emissions over time with the data sources and 
methods used by SANDAG. Consistent emissions tracking is essential to verify the effectiveness of 
the program and policy implementation for the CAP Update. The use of an updated emissions 
inventory baseline also informs development of updated GHG emissions forecasts and reduction 
targets consistent with anticipated growth in the City and State legislation. 
 
1  The GHG emissions inventory provided through ReCAP is a “communitywide” emissions inventory, which covers all relevant GHG 
emissions sources and/or activities in the City of Oceanside boundary. Municipal government operations are considered within this 
communitywide inventory but are not explicitly disaggregated. 


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
3-2 
The 2018 GHG emissions inventory used in the CAP Update was provided to the City through the 
ReCAP program. This was the most recent year regional GHG data were available from SANDAG 
prior to the COVID-19 pandemic which substantially altered transportation patterns. It has been 
modified to provide consistency with the General Plan Update land uses and to reflect relevant 
emissions sources and/or activities in Oceanside. Vehicle miles traveled (VMT) data, which is used 
to calculate on-road transportation emissions, was updated from that used in the 2018 inventory 
as provided by the ReCAP Snapshot to utilize a land use and growth scenario consistent with the 
General Plan Update. The purpose of this exercise is to better reflect how GHG emissions may 
change in the future under implementation of the City’s General Plan. Additionally, emissions from 
the use of off-road equipment use have been added to the GHG inventory, which was not included 
in the 2018 ReCAP Snapshot inventory. These updates provide a more comprehensive assessment 
of emissions that allows for alignment with expected future growth.  
2018 Greenhouse Gas Inventory Update 
The 2018 communitywide baseline GHG emissions inventory demonstrates the contribution of 
different GHG emissions sources in Oceanside by emissions sector. The largest contributor to total 
emissions was fuel combustion in on-road vehicles, representing 51 percent of total emissions. The 
next two largest emissions sources were grid-purchased electricity and natural gas use in buildings 
and facilities within the City, contributing 21 percent and 19 percent to total emissions, 
respectively. The remaining emissions sectors, including solid waste, off-road transportation, 
water, and wastewater represent approximately nine (9) percent of total emissions. In total, 
communitywide emissions sources considered in the 2018 emissions inventory generated 
approximately 845,000 metric tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (MTCO2e). These results are 
presented in Figure 3-1 and Table 3-1, showing the total emissions by sector with the 
representative contribution to total communitywide emissions. All values presented in this chapter 
are rounded to the nearest thousand; therefore, totals in tables may not sum precisely. More detail 
on the results and methods for the 2018 emissions inventory can be found in Appendix A.

3.3 
GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS FORECASTS 
The GHG emissions forecasts estimate how communitywide GHG emissions for Oceanside may 
change given future increases in housing and economic growth, transportation modes and how 
State and Federal regulations may help to reduce local emissions. Forecast results provide insight 
into changes in future emissions levels and the type and scale of actions that will be needed to align 
with State GHG reduction targets. The CAP Update includes two forecasts to represent future GHG 
conditions with and without the impact of local, State, and Federal regulations as described below.  
The GHG emissions forecasts are presented as a sector-level assessment of near-term (2030) and 
long-term (2045 and 2050) GHG emissions projections based on current conditions under two 
scenarios. Under the first scenario, GHG emissions would grow from 2018 levels at the same rates 
as housing, population, employment, and vehicle travel, which is known as a business-as-usual 
(BAU) scenario forecast. The BAU forecast serves as a basis for understanding how emissions levels 
may change with growth, and how far GHG emissions will need to be reduced in future years to 
meet GHG reduction targets without considering future action to reduce GHG emissions. The 
second scenario considers the GHG reduction impact of local, State, and Federal regulations, which 
is known as a legislative adjusted business-as-usual scenario (ABAU) forecast. The ABAU forecast 
provides a more detailed assessment of future GHG emissions by showing how currently adopted 
local, State and Federal regulations will help reduce emissions in Oceanside. The future years 
selected for the GHG emissions forecasts are aligned with the GHG reduction target years under 
State legislation, 2030 for Senate Bill (SB) 32 and 2045 for Assembly Bill (AB) 1279, as well as 2050 
to align with the in-progress General Plan Update horizon year.

Business-as-Usual GHG Emissions Forecast  
The BAU forecast was developed by scaling communitywide GHG emissions from the 2018 
emissions inventory based on the City’s growth in housing, jobs, and vehicle travel. More detail on 
the approach for forecasting specific GHG emissions sectors is provided in Appendix A. VMT for on-
road transportation is also essential for understanding how future GHG emissions may change in 
Oceanside. VMT was modeled using the SANDAG ABM2+ model version 14.3.0, with adjustments 
made to account for the growth in housing and overall development consistent with the land uses 
included in the City’s in-progress General Plan Update.  
The results of the BAU forecast show that communitywide GHG emissions would be expected to 
grow, given no further GHG reduction efforts beyond 2018, as shown in Table3-2. While a more 
likely scenario for future GHG emissions can be seen in the ABAU forecast, the BAU scenario 
provides the basis for understanding the GHG impact of anticipated growth in the City.

Legislative Adjusted Business-as-Usual GHG Emissions Forecast  
The ABAU forecast was developed by accounting for the expected GHG reductions in 
communitywide GHG emissions in Oceanside as a result of adopted local, State, and Federal 
regulations. The GHG reductions considered in the ABAU forecast fall into three categories: 
building energy efficiency standards, fuel efficiency standards, electric vehicle sale requirements, 
and renewable electricity requirements. The full list of legislation considered in the ABAU forecast 
is provided in Appendix A. In addition, since the development of the 2019 CAP, the City of 
Oceanside joined the Clean Energy Alliance (CEA), a publicly-managed Joint Powers Authority that 
procures renewable power on behalf of its member agencies. In April 2024, all electricity accounts 
in Oceanside were enrolled into CEA’s service, providing electrical power comprised of at least 50 
percent renewable sources. The ABAU scenario considers the GHG reductions associated with this 
change in power mix now delivered to Oceanside customers.

3.4 
GREENHOUSE GAS REDUCTION TARGETS 
A primary purpose of the CAP Update is to align the City of Oceanside’s GHG reduction targets with 
the most recent State legislation. Since the 2019 CAP was adopted, AB 1279 was signed into law, 
which requires the State to develop and implement a strategy for achieving statewide net-zero 
emissions by 2045 or sooner, which includes an 85 percent reduction in anthropogenic GHG 
emissions by 2045 with remaining emissions offset by carbon dioxide removal. The state’s strategy 
for meeting the AB 1279 target, as outlined in the 2022 Climate Change Scoping Plan, indicates that 
net-zero emissions would be achieved through CO2 removal through both carbon sequestration in 
natural lands and mechanical carbon capture and storage technologies. Carbon sequestration and 
CO2 removal and carbon capture and storage technologies are being evaluated by the State, but it 
is not feasible for local jurisdictions such as Oceanside to scale these solutions independently to 
achieve net-zero GHG emissions by 2045. The City is, however, working to identify and advance 
projects with natural carbon sequestration benefits and potential through watershed and wetland 
restoration that also improve coastal ecosystem function.

3-7 
To align with recent State legislation, the CAP Update includes a long-term GHG reduction target 
in alignment with AB 1279. The 2030 GHG reduction target of the CAP update is maintaining 
consistency with the 2019 CAP by aligning with SB 32. Additionally, a 2050 target is being added to 
align with the in-progress General Plan Update horizon year.  
Aligning with Statewide Targets 
The primary Statewide GHG reduction targets relevant to the CAP Update are those established 
by SB 32 of 2016 and AB 1279 of 2022, which include: 
• 
Reducing statewide GHG emissions 40 percent below 1990 levels by 2030 (SB 32), and 
• 
Reducing statewide anthropogenic GHG emissions 85 percent below 1990 levels before 2045 
(AB 1279). 
With the State’s adoption of these legislative targets, the California Air Resources Board (CARB) 
was directed to develop and implement strategies to reduce statewide emissions consistent with 
the targets through a Climate Change Scoping Plan (Scoping Plan). The Scoping Plan outlines the 
State’s strategy to reduce GHG emissions in each economic sector and is regularly updated to 
reflect the most recent trends, technology, and legislative reduction targets. California’s 2017 
Climate Change Scoping Plan was developed and adopted to outline the State’s strategy for reducing 
GHG emissions to meet the SB 32 legislative reduction target. The most recent Scoping Plan, The 
2022 Scoping Plan for Achieving Carbon Neutrality, was similarly developed for meeting the AB 1279 
GHG reduction target. The pathways for statewide GHG emissions reductions in both versions of 
the Scoping Plan provide a framework for developing local GHG reduction targets.  
Greenhouse Gas Reduction Targets for Oceanside 
The Scoping Plan identifies local governments as essential partners in achieving California’s GHG 
reduction goals. One way for local governments to support the State goals is to establish GHG 
reduction targets that align with State targets and the strategies outlined in the Scoping Plan. The 
City of Oceanside has aligned its local GHG reduction targets in the CAP Update with the Scoping 
Plan’s future GHG emissions scenarios for economic sectors that are relevant to local conditions. 
This allows the City to set GHG reduction targets that are directly aligned with the State’s strategies 
for reducing GHG emissions and are based on a more recent GHG inventory year.  
Since the State compares its GHG reductions targets against a 1990 baseline, and the City of 
Oceanside does not have a 1990 GHG inventory available, local targets were calculated based on 
the State’s historical GHG emissions levels and the future pathways of the Scoping Plans. More 
detail on how Oceanside’s GHG reduction targets were calculated, and alignment with the Scoping 
Plan GHG emissions sectors and modeling, is available in Appendix A. 
The GHG reduction targets for the CAP Update demonstrate consistency with State legislation 
through locally specific GHG target levels based on the City’s 2018 GHG emissions inventory. The 
GHG reduction targets are aligned with the key statewide reduction targets and target years of SB 32 


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
3-8 
and AB 1279, as well as an additional long-term target for the year 2050 to align with the in-progress 
General Plan Update horizon year. The GHG reduction targets for the CAP Update include: 
• 
Reducing emissions 27 percent below 2018 levels by 2030 (aligned with SB 32),  
• 
Reducing emissions 90 percent below 2018 levels by 2045 (aligned with AB 1279), and 
• 
Maintaining emissions 90 percent below 2018 levels through 2050 (General Plan Update 
horizon year). 
The City of Oceanside’s updated GHG reduction targets for the years 2030, 2045, and 2050 are 
provided in Table 3-5.

3.5 
REACHING THE TARGETS 
The purpose of the CAP Update is to develop strategies the City can implement to reduce 
communitywide GHG emissions consistent with local reduction targets. With the GHG emissions 
forecasts and reduction targets presented in this chapter, the City can assess the GHG reductions 
needed to achieve the targets by bridging the “emissions gap.” The emissions gap is the difference 
between future emissions from the ABAU forecast and the additional reductions needed to meet 
the targets. The measures and actions described in the CAP Update are necessary for the City to 
achieve its targets in alignment with the State’s goals. Table 3-6 and Figure 3-3 provide a summary 
of the ABAU forecast in comparison with the updated GHG reduction targets, and the emissions 
gap that would need to be reduced to meet the targets.
