CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
3-10 
The City of Oceanside is not expected to meet its GHG reduction targets through State and Federal 
legislation alone. Locally implemented GHG reduction measures will be needed to meet the targets. 
These local measures are outlined in this CAP Update. The GHG reduction measures of the CAP 
Update build upon the successes of the 2019 CAP and provide a pathway for the deep GHG 
reductions needed to align with State GHG reduction targets. These reduction measures are 
outlined in the following chapter.  


 
GREENHOUSE GAS REDUCTION 
4 
4-1 
4 
Greenhouse Gas Reduction  
Measures and Actions 
4.1 
OVERVIEW 
The City of Oceanside (City) seeks to assist its residents, workers, and visitors in rendering their 
everyday activities more sustainable and less carbon-intensive. With this vision, the City has 
developed a suite of greenhouse gas (GHG) reduction measures and actions that will reduce the 
carbon-intensity of municipal operations, expand existing City programs to support the community 
in reducing its carbon footprint, and establish new building codes that reduce energy use and 
expand access to renewable energy in new and existing development. All of these measures and 
actions are designed to facilitate the decarbonization of the local economy. Together, these 
measures and actions strike a balance between the need to meet long-term GHG reduction targets 
and the resources available to effectively implement measures. This chapter presents the GHG 
reduction measures and actions that are the core of the Climate Action Plan Update (CAP Update).  
The following sections outline the GHG reduction framework developed for this CAP Update and 
summarize the factors that shaped the framework. Further on, the chapter presents a summary of 
each GHG reduction measure and the associated actions for implementation.  
4.2 
GREENHOUSE GAS REDUCTION FRAMEWORK 
The GHG reduction measures identified in this CAP Update are organized under six focus sectors. 
These sectors generally align with the 2018 GHG emissions inventory sources, but they are not 
exactly the same as the emission inventory sectors. This is because some measures are cross-
cutting in nature, while others are intended to provide additional community benefits while 
reducing emissions from sources not accounted for in the City’s GHG emissions inventory. The six 
focus sectors under which the GHG reduction measures are organized are listed in Figure 4-1 along 
with a brief description of the type of measures included in the focus sector. 
Measures in each sector will be implemented through discrete implementation actions. 
Implementation actions are labeled as either “key” or “supportive” to highlight how the actions 
under each measure interact. Key actions are core to the success of the GHG reduction measure as 
a whole and are tied directly to the GHG reductions that are anticipated to be achieved from those 
measures. Supportive actions are more focused towards supporting the City and community 
members in implementing and tracking progress of each measure. Both action types work together 
to provide transparency in how GHG reduction measures can be successfully implemented. 
Additionally, some actions have been labeled as “inclusive,” which highlights where equity 
considerations have been integrated to ensure that the economic, environmental, and social 
benefits of measures extend to more community members and that potential negative impacts on 
low-income or historically disadvantaged communities are minimized. 
 


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
ENERGY 
Includes measures that reduce emissions in the natural 
gas and electricity GHG inventory sectors, which 
primarily encompasses energy used in buildings. 
TRANSPORTATION 
Includes measures that reduce emissions in the on-
road transportation and off-road transportation GHG 
inventory sectors, focusing on reducing vehicle travel 
and shifting vehicles and equipment to electricity and 
renewable fuels. 
SOLID WASTE 
Includes measures that reduce emissions resulting from 
the decay of organic materials in landfills, including 
increasing waste diversion and composting. 
WATER 
Includes measures that reduce water consumption and 
the electricity emissions associated with the City’s 
water supply. 
CARBON 
SEQUESTRATION 
Reduces the City’s overall GHG emissions contribution 
by protecting natural lands and expanding the urban 
forests.

Intended to reduce emissions from consumer products 
and support local job growth in emerging sectors.

combination of factors, including the results of the City’s GHG emissions inventories and forecasts, 
feedback from the public and stakeholders, and the best climate action planning guidance available 
to date. Figure 4-2 describes the hierarchy and provides definitions for sectors, measures, and 
actions developed for the CAP.

4.3 
GHG REDUCTION SUMMARY 
In preparation of the CAP Update, a GHG quantification analysis was conducted to estimate 
potential emissions reductions associated with all measures. Implementation of the measures is 
estimated to result in 55,516 metric tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (MTCO2e) of GHG 
reductions in 2030; 239,346 MTCO2e in 2045; and 226,575 MTCO2e in 2050. The quantification 
analysis demonstrated that the total estimated reductions from all GHG reduction measures would 
be sufficient to meet the 2030 target, consistent with State goals. However, the analysis shows that 
the quantified measures may not be sufficient to meet the 2045 and 2050 targets. The results of 
the quantification analysis are shown in Table 4-1 below.

Notes: Totals may not sum exactly due to independent rounding. PV = photovoltaic; MTCO2e = metric tons of carbon 
dioxide equivalent; MW = megawatts; NA = not applicable; TDM = transportation demand management; VMT = 
vehicle miles traveled; ZEV = zero emission vehicle. 
Parentheses indicate target was met with a surplus of reductions. 
While the GHG reduction targets for 2045 and 2050 are not met with quantified measures alone, 
the measures presented here are intended to be feasible to implement and administer with current 
City resources and cost-effective for community members. The measures have been developed to 
set the City on a pathway that aligns with the statewide GHG reduction targets and demonstrate 
alignment with the State’s key priorities of building electrification, preserving natural lands, infill-
focused development, and expanding zero-emission vehicle access. The quantified GHG reductions 
for measures have been developed to ensure that near-term implementation actions can be 
substantially supported. However, some measures include longer-term actions that are supportive 
of the State’s priorities but have not been quantified due to a lack of data or the inability to define 
programs in sufficient detail at this time. The City will monitor progress towards meeting near- and 
long-term reduction targets and update the CAP regularly to evaluate how new technologies, 
funding opportunities, and State and Federal legislation may support the City in reaching long-term 
CAP goals. The City will also explore additional measures and expansion of programs as GHG 
reduction trends are monitored over time. More detail on the City’s monitoring procedures and 
potential expansion of measures is provided in Chapter 5 Implementation and Monitoring.

Reduction Measures Technical Memorandum for the City of Oceanside Climate Action Plan Update. 
Energy 
GHG emissions from energy consumption and generation represented 40% of the City’s total 
emissions in 2018. Even as electricity-related emissions are reduced in the future through delivery 
of renewable electricity by Clean Energy Alliance (CEA), natural gas consumption will continue to 
represent a significant proportion of the City’s total GHG emissions through 2045 and 2050. The 
GHG reduction measures and actions included in the Energy sector strive to promote energy 
efficiency, increase renewable energy availability, and reduce future consumption of natural gas. 
Measure E-1 
Provide 100% renewable/zero-carbon energy delivery through the 
Clean Energy Alliance (CEA) by 2030 with a 95% participation rate. 
Year 
2030 
2045 
2050 
GHG Reduction 
Potential (MTCO2e) 
11,358 
0 
0 
To reduce emissions associated with electricity generation, the City will increase the share of 
renewable energy delivered to Oceanside customers. CEA, a community choice aggregation (CCA) 
program, currently provides Oceanside residents and businesses with a minimum of 50% renewable 
/ 75% zero-carbon electricity sources as a default for those who do not opt-out of CEA service. The 
City can further the impact of joining CEA by increasing the default electricity rate to 100% renewable 
electricity for accounts in the community. To demonstrate its commitment to sustainability and 
achieving the CAP goals, the City will also enroll all municipal operations electricity accounts in the 
100% renewable/zero-carbon option, informed by cost estimates of such a transition. Regular 
outreach campaigns will be conducted to encourage continued participation in CEA, which includes 
sharing information online and promoting CEA participation at community events.

customers in the community to 100% renewable/zero-carbon energy

Annually track CEA participation rates, including opt-out and opt-down

increasing participation.

renewable energy choices and programs through print, social media and

Measure E-2 
Increase behind-the-meter solar photovoltaic (PV) capacity in the City 
to 125 megawatts (MW) by 2030 and 165 MW by 2045. 
Year

To enhance the resilience of the City's energy infrastructure 
and reduce reliance on grid supplied electricity, the City 
plans to increase the availability of local renewable energy in 
Oceanside. Through incremental expansion of the PV 
capacity at both City- and privately-owned properties, the 
City plans to achieve at least 125 MW of communitywide 
solar capacity by 2030 and 165 MW by 2045. As of May 
2025, 114 MW of communitywide solar capacity had been 
installed (CPUC 2025). 
Residential Solar 


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
The City will ensure the expansion of solar infrastructure 
through the adoption of both the State’s triennial updates to 
the Building Energy Efficiency Standards (Title 24, Part 6) and 
also consider local building code amendments (‘reach codes’) 
that enhance solar PV requirements on all new construction 
and at City-owned facilities.  
The City will also support the voluntary installation of solar 
infrastructure through an expedited permitting process, 
promotion of financing options, and collaboration with CEA to 
support installation of solar at low-income households. 
Measure E-2 Implementing Actions 
Action E-2.1 
By 2027, adopt a building code standard that requires solar PV systems 
on all new qualifying residential and non-residential construction and 
qualifying major renovations. This may be accomplished either through 
the 2025 Building Energy Efficiency Standards or a reach code designed 
to exceed the 2025 Building Energy Efficiency Standards so that all new 
construction has solar PV incorporated into the design where feasible. 
Action E-2.2 
Implement energy efficiency upgrades and CIP projects at City facilities 
per the turnkey Energy Services Contract with Opterra that was 
adopted by the City Council in January 2024. 
Action E-2.3 
Promote current and future CEA incentives and rebate programs that 
encourage low-cost solar and battery storage upgrades for households, 
including rebates for income-qualified customers that are funded 
through CEA residual revenues. 
Action E-2.4 
Promote available rooftop solar rebates and financing options through 
entities other than CEA, such as San Diego Gas & Electric (SDGE), the 
California Solar Initiative (CSI), Self-Generation Incentive Program 
(SGIP), the SGIP Equity Resiliency Rebate Program, and the Federal 
Solar Tax Credit Program. 
Action E-2.5 
Maintain expedited permitting for residential solar PV systems.

Measure E-3 
Launch an “Energy Upgrade Oceanside Program” with a goal of 
achieving 400 building energy efficiency retrofits by 2030 and 60,000 
by 2045 with primary end uses converted to electric. 
Year 
2030 
2045 
2050 
GHG Reduction 
Potential (MTCO2e) 
632 
171,520 
171,520 
To reduce emissions from energy use in existing buildings and support more efficient homes and 
businesses, the City will develop and launch an “Energy Upgrade Oceanside Program” to 
strategically transition Oceanside towards more efficient and primarily electricity-powered 
buildings. Initial steps would include adoption of building “reach codes1” to require electrical 
retrofits and renovations of specified scope to achieve total building energy efficiency levels based 
on a scoring criterion. Future reach code implementation will be supported through additional 
funding opportunities and sharing information on cost-saving opportunities such as tax credits and 
rebates. Longer-term decarbonization of buildings will require broader strategies and mechanisms 
to support electrification of existing buildings that does not increase costs to residents and 
businesses. To this end, the City will develop a detailed existing building decarbonization strategy 
that analyzes the specific challenges and opportunities for electrification in Oceanside and 
develops a phased long-term strategy for retrofitting the majority of buildings in the city. To ensure 
equitable implementation of measures and avoid unintended 
consequences, the City will track data related to energy upgrades 
and associated tenant displacement or disproportionate rent 
increases. If detrimental impacts are found to occur, the City will 
assess the feasibility of developing anti-displacement actions. As 
part of the Energy Upgrade Oceanside Program and its long-term 
energy services contract with Opterra, the City will also retrofit 
its own buildings and facilities to improve efficiency and switch to 
electric end-uses where feasible and cost effective. 
1 Reach codes are amendments to the State’s Building Energy Efficiency Standards (“Energy Code” – Title 24, Part 6) or the California 
Green Building Standards Code (“CALGreen” – Title 24, Part 11) adopted at the local level to exceed minimum requirements for energy 
efficiency, GHG emissions and sustainable practices in building design and construction.  


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
4-10 
Measure E-3 Implementing Actions 
Action E-3.1 
Develop an “Existing Building Energy Conservation Ordinance” 
requiring existing buildings with qualifying improvements (addition of 
750 square feet [sf] for residential, 1,500 sf or 15% above existing sf 
for non-residential) to concurrently implement energy efficient 
upgrades. Provide options to select and implement efficiency 
measures from a prescriptive list, which collectively achieve an 
efficiency level based on a scoring criterion. The analysis should 
identify which efficiency measures are considered cost effective for 
different building use types.  
 
Action E-3.2 
Adopt the “Existing Building Conservation Ordinance” consistent with 
the results of a cost-effectiveness analysis in Action E-3.1 by 2027.  
 
Action E-3.3 
Develop a tracking system for the types of energy efficiency upgrades 
completed by applicants from the “Existing Building Conservation 
Ordinance” within the existing permit database software, including 
building primary use, annual energy consumption, square footage of 
retrofits, types of upgrades completed, and end uses electrified.  
 
Action E-3.4 
Every three years, following adoption of the “Existing Building 
Conservation Ordinance,” review the ordinance’s effectiveness in 
encouraging residents to perform cost-positive energy conservation 
measures and increase the stringency of the program or adjust the 
threshold of qualifying improvements as needed to reduce 
communitywide energy consumption. Reviews and updates to the 
ordinance will be coordinated to align with the triennial State Building 
Standards Code update cycle. 
 
Action E-3.5 
Support proposed General Plan (GP) policy HLC 7-142 by establishing 
a revolving loan fund to provide low-interest loans to low- and fixed-
income residents to cover the costs of time-of-replacement 
electrification upgrades, which may include replacement of hot-water 
heaters and heating, ventilation, and air conditioning (HVAC) units with 
electric heat pump options. Provide that the loans can be processed in 
reduced timeframes to the extent feasible to enable replacement of 
equipment in a timely manner and that funds cover the cost of electric 
equipment replacements, including panel upgrades if needed. Use 
development mitigation fees, CEA residual revenue, U.S. Department 
of Housing and Development’s (HUD's) Green and Resilient Retrofit 
Program (GRRP)'s Grants and Loans component and other sources for 
capitalizing the revolving loan fund. Implement a targeted outreach 
campaign with loan establishment that allows residents ready access to 
program information (e.g., magnets or mailers).

By 2030, develop an Existing Building Decarbonization Strategy that

Oceanside and develops a phased long-term strategy for retrofitting 
the majority of buildings in the City without increasing costs for 
residents and businesses. 
Measure E-4 
Require that all new construction be electric ready by 2026. 
Year 
2030 
2045 
2050 
GHG Reduction 
Potential (MTCO2e) 
894 
6,213 
7,848 
To reduce building energy dependence on fossil fuels in the future, the City plans to adopt and enforce 
the upcoming 2025 Building Energy Efficiency Standards. For single family residential buildings, the 
anticipated State standards include heat pump space heaters as well as heat pump water heaters or 
solar water heating system as prescriptive requirements in all climate zones. The proposed standards 
set heat pump water heaters as the prescriptive baseline for individual systems serving individual 
dwelling units in multifamily buildings with three or fewer habitable stories. The proposed standards 
also include mandatory requirements for electric ready multifamily buildings. Specifically, the 
standards include requirements to ensure that the entire building’s electrical systems have sufficient 
capacity to supply full rated amperage for all electric ready appliances, including heat pump space 
heaters, electric cooktops and electric clothes dryers in accordance with the California Electrical 


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
Code. Ultimately, if the adopted standards deviate from the requirements outlined here, the City will 
need to consider adoption of reach codes that achieve comparable reductions.  
These requirements applied to new construction will reduce building energy-related emissions and 
prepare the city for future electrification efforts. Through this measure, the City intends to make 
the transition to electric power easier for the community and ensure long-term cost savings for 
residents and businesses. The City plans to respond to the increased demand for contractors, 
technicians, and construction workers with expertise in electrical systems by partnering with 
regional organizations and providing training to the local workforce. Additionally, all newly 
constructed City facilities will be all electric, starting in 2030. 
Measure E-4 Implementing Actions 
Action E-4.1 
To support proposed GP goal ECLU 3-G, in 2025, adopt the 2025 Building 
Energy Efficiency Standards to support electrification and electric 
readiness in new buildings. If the 2025 Building Energy Efficiency 
Standards deviate from the proposals outlined above, the City will 
evaluate a reach code designed to meet the standards as proposed. 
Action E-4.2 
Partner with San Diego Building Trades Council (SDBTC), San Diego 
Workforce Partnership (SDWP), San Diego Regional Energy Network 
(SDREN) and San Diego LEARN program to provide workforce training 
and contractor engagement on the requirements for all-electric new 
construction prior to and after ordinance adoption. Market training 
opportunities through the permitting counter, the City’s website, and the 
Sustainable Business network. 
Action E-4.3 
Support proposed GP policy HLC 7-141 by requiring the design and 
construction of all new City facilities to be all-electric from 2026, with 
exemption for end uses that do not have feasible electric alternatives at 
the time of design.

Transportation 
Transportation was the largest GHG emissions source in the City in 2018, representing 51% of total 
emissions, and will continue to be the largest GHG emissions source through 2045 and 2050. The 
key lever the City has to reduce transportation-related emissions is through a reduction in vehicle 
miles traveled (VMT) and facilitating the increased adoption of zero emission vehicles and 
equipment, which are the focus of the measures and actions in the Transportation sector. 
Measure TR-1 
Reduce per-capita vehicle miles traveled (VMT) 3% by 2030 and 6% 
by 2045 from 2016 levels through Transportation Demand Management 
(TDM) strategies. 
Year 
2030 
2045 
2050 
GHG Reduction 
Potential (MTCO2e) 
22 
21 
15 
To reduce emissions associated with vehicular travel, the City plans to require and support TDM 
strategies to reduce VMT through multiple actions. A primary action is to lower the compliance 
threshold for the City’s current TDM Ordinance to 40 daily commute trips for businesses. This will 
serve to capture additional employers and increase the effectiveness of the ordinance. The City will 
support employers in complying with these new requirements by providing model TDM plans and 
providing information about cost effective TDM measures, such as utilizing the San Diego 
Association of Governments Sustainable Transportation Services program. The City will also 
continue to promote, implement, and expand micro-transit options and support City employee 
commute trip reductions. The North County Transit District (NCTD) plans to launch a pilot micro-
transit program called NCTD+ in north county cities. NCTD+ is an on-demand transit service that 
provides shared rides within a service zone. NCTD+ complements existing public transit and offers 
first and last mile connections to rail stations and transit centers. The first service zone is planned 
to launch in June 2024 in San Marcos, with potential expansion to Oceanside in 2026, as funding is 
available.  


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
Measure TR-1 Implementing Actions 
Action TR-1.1 
By 2027 amend the Transportation Demand Management Ordinance 
(Zoning Ordinance 3050) to require businesses that generate 40 or 
more daily commute trips to comply with the ordinance. Zoning 
Ordinance 3050 will also be amended to be applicable to businesses 
that generate 40 or more daily commute trips in redevelopment 
projects and at sites where a conditional use permit is being 
issued/updated. 
Action TR-1.2 
Develop a model TDM plan and provide education on compliance and 
reporting and resources for implementing TDM measures for 
businesses subject to Zoning Ordinance 3050. 
Action TR-1.3 
Support proposed GP policies IM 4-85 and 4-86 by implementing an 
outreach and educational campaign targeted to businesses to promote 
TDM strategies and encourage participation in the San Diego 
Association of Governments’ (SANDAG’s) Sustainable Transportation 
Services program. 
Action TR-1.4 
Reduce City employee VMT by providing subsidized transit passes, a 
carpool connection program, preferential parking for carpools, and 
setting pricing for employee parking to provide parking cash out 
opportunities. 
Action TR-1.5 
Support GP policy IM4-72 by partnering with SANDAG on Flexible 
Fleets initiatives such as neighborhood electric vehicles (NEV) and 
North County Transit District (NCTD) in the implementation of the 
NCTD+ on-demand microtransit program to operate in various areas 
in the city, including sections of the Smart and Sustainable Corridors 
Specific Plan planning area. 
Action TR-1.6 
Support proposed GP policies ECLU 3-43 and 3-45 by requiring that 
new mixed-use developments promote linkages with adjacent uses 
and provide adequate and secure bike parking facilities, as well as 
allowing for mobility, transit accessibility, and telework amenities as a 
part of public benefits packages.

(iii) providing opportunities for cash fare payment to provide

Measure TR-3 
Increase zero emission vehicle (ZEV) adoption to 20% by 2030 and 
90% by 2045 through charging infrastructure deployment. 
Year

Through this measure, the City aims to increase ZEV adoption by improving the EV charging 
infrastructure in Oceanside, which would reduce emissions generated by driving gasoline- and diesel-
fueled vehicles. The City plans to adopt ordinances to require EV charging facilities at new and 
modified gas stations and commercial centers, as well as auxiliary power at delivery docks for reduced 
truck idling. The City will also develop a strategy to transition the municipal fleet to ZEVs by 2045 as 
required by California Air Resources Board 
regulations. To support the community in the 
transition to ZEVs the City will promote 
incentives offered by various agencies and 
complete a communitywide ZEV Infrastructure 
Deployment Strategy. Measure TR-3 is intended 
to support the implementation of the State’s ZEV 
sale requirements of the Advanced Clean Cars 
(ACC) II and Advanced Clean Fleets (ACF) rules. 
ChargePoint EV charging

Measure TR -3 Implementing Actions 
Action TR-3.1 
Support proposed GP policy ECLU 3-86 by adopting an ordinance to 
require the installation of EV direct current (DC) fast chargers at: 
(i) 
All new gas stations and 
(ii) Existing gas stations that undergo a proposed amendment to the 
conditional use permit (CUP) that includes modifications to the 
storage tanks, expansions to associated convenience stores, or 
modifications/additions to service components (e.g., car washes). 
 
Action TR-3.2 
Consistent with proposed GP policy ECLU 3-15, adopt an ordinance 
requiring EV charging facilities that meet California Green Building 
Standards Code (CALGreen) Tier 2 standards at commercial centers 
that are: 
(i) 
Newly constructed and 
(ii) Performing parking lot resurfacing. 
 
Action TR-3.3 
Adopt an ordinance to require all new nonresidential development with 
loading docks to supply sufficient electrical power for delivery trucks and 
associated equipment to reduce idling when making deliveries.  
 
Action TR-3.4 
Support proposed GP policy VSR 5-66 by conducting an inventory of 
the existing EV chargers and vehicles in the City vehicle fleet and 
developing a Zero Emission Vehicles Transition Plan with a goal to: 
(i) 
Electrify 60% municipal fleet by 2030 and 100% of all feasible 
vehicles and equipment by 2045,

properties (Civic Center, Libraries, Senior & Community Centers, 
Parks, Swimming Pools) by 2030.

The City’s General Plan Update and other existing adopted City documents include policies that 
focus on Smart Growth principles 2 that are intended to support and incentivize growth and 
mobility in priority infill areas. This urban planning concept is an essential component of the City's 
strategy for reducing per-capita VMT and meeting local GHG reduction targets. This concerted 
effort across multiple General Plan elements stages a future where residents and workers in 
Oceanside commute more efficiently, and with less reliance on vehicle travel. The General Plan 
elements that include policies contributing to this vision for Oceanside include: 
•
Efficient and Compatible Land Use (ECLU) Element,
•
Integrated Mobility (IM) Element, and
•
Smart and Sustainable Corridors Specific Plan (SSCSP).
These proposed General Plan Update elements are in addition to the Coast Highway Vision and 
Strategic Plan, which promotes smart growth principles and future complete streets upgrades to 
the Coast Highway Corridor project area, supported via the Coast Highway Incentive District 
(CHID). The overall goal is to concentrate new urban development west of Interstate 5 along the 
Coast Highway Corridor and create a more walkable and accessible environment for residents, 
workers and visitors.  
2 Smart growth principles in urban planning involves applying development policy that results in compact, livable, efficient and 
environmentally sensitive development patterns with a focus on public transit use and accessibility.  
4-22


 
GREENHOUSE GAS REDUCTION 
4 
4-23 
The combination of these General Plan elements and plans provides the City with a growth 
framework that prioritizes infill and neighborhood scale developments with complete streets 
principles that enable access to existing transit and essential services through walking, biking, and 
micro mobility options. The City intends to incentivize development in these priority areas by 
streamlining the development review process for projects that align with form-based standards and 
include community benefit features.

Water 
The Water sector represents a small proportion of the City’s total emissions, only 1% in 2018; 
however, the City has significant control over this emissions source through the ability to utilize 
local water supplies, reducing demand, and utilizing low-carbon electricity sources for pumping, 
treating, and delivering water to customers.  
 
Measure W-1 
Reduce per-capita water consumption in the City by 6% by 2030, 
continued through 2045, compared to 2018 levels.  
Year 
2030 
2045 
2050 
GHG Reduction 
Potential (MTCO2e) 
205 
0 
0 
Through this measure, the City aims to reduce per-capita water consumption and in turn reduce the 
upstream electricity emissions associated with the water sector by implementing a Water 
Conservation Plan, City Facilities Water Efficiency Plan, and by implementing its Model Water 
Efficient Landscape Ordinance (MWELO). Through actions like water-efficient building practices, 
low-water landscaping, plumbing code updates and outreach efforts, the City plans a phased 
reduction in per-capita water consumption by 2045. 
Additionally, the City plans on adopting ordinances that 
require water-efficient fixtures in new renovated buildings 
consistent with the State’s CALGreen building standards, 
distributing water conservation kits to low-income 
families, promoting water use monitoring and installing 
low-water use landscaping in City-owned buildings. To 
support the community in water use efficiency practices, 
the City plans to continue offering rebates on water-
efficient fixtures and use Green Oceanside for maintaining 
outreach efforts. 
Measure W -1 Implementing Actions 
Action W-1.1 
By 2030, develop and implement a City Facilities Water Efficiency Plan 
to require water-efficiency measures in new and existing City 
buildings/operations to reduce potable water use by 20% by 2030 
compared to 2018 baseline water use.

Solid Waste 
The Solid Waste sector of emissions represents a small proportion of the City’s total emissions, only 
4% in 2018; with emissions resulting from the decay of organic materials in landfills over time, and 
energy used for landfilling operations. The emissions reduction measures and actions presented 
here focus on diverting waste from landfills through increased composting capacity, food waste 
recovery, and overall waste diversion.

Carbon Sequestration 
Enhancing and protecting the carbon sequestration potential of natural lands, watersheds and the 
urban forest in Oceanside are key to supporting the State’s “30x30” initiative to protect 30% of the 
state’s natural lands and coastal waters by 2030. Relying on natural solutions to mitigate climate 
change can also enhance the City’s resiliency to climate change hazards. 
Carbon Cycle

Measure CS-1 
Plant 200 new trees (net) citywide, to achieve 800 new trees (net) by 
2030, and 3,800 by 2045 while maintaining the existing urban tree 
canopy. 
Year 
2030 
2045 
2050 
GHG Reduction 
Potential (MTCO2e) 
1,726 
10,356 
13,232 
Through this measure, the City plans to sequester carbon through tree planting in Oceanside to 
increase the urban forest canopy. The City will achieve this by adopting a Green Streets Policy for 
development projects, in addition to existing urban forestry development standards, and 
encouraging voluntary tree planting on private property. Compensatory measures for tree removal 
during construction will be adopted to avoid or minimize tree removal or plan the appropriate 
replacement of removed trees. This measure enhances the City’s existing urban forestry and site 
permeability requirements in its zoning ordinance for development projects. The City may also 
explore an in-lieu fee program for financing future tree planting activities, where development 
projects cannot meet the tree canopy standards. The measure aims to enhance environmental 
sustainability and health benefits for the community.

Measure CS 1 Implementing Actions 
Action CS-1.1 
Implement a Citywide program to plant 800 shade trees by 2030 and 
3,800 shade trees by 2045 in City-owned parks, buildings, facilities, 
roadway projects, and on private property, while preserving the 
existing tree canopy. Underserved neighborhoods that lack tree 
canopy will be prioritized for tree planting efforts.  
 
Action CS-1.2 
Require development projects to compensate for tree removal during 
construction. Prior to the removal of any trees, projects shall obtain a 
tree removal permit, and if necessary, develop a tree avoidance, 
minimization, and replacement plan in consultation with a certified 
arborist or the City of Oceanside’s Department of Public Works. 
Replacement plantings should be native species where practicable. 
Invasive species (as defined by the California Invasive Plant Council) 
shall not be planted.  
 
Action CS-1.3 
Continue to implement Zoning Ordinance 3049, which requires all 
new development subject to discretionary review to comply with 
urban forestry standards.  
 
Action CS-1.4 
Complete a nexus study and establish a Tree Fund into which all new 
development subject to compliance with Zoning Ordinance 3049 can 
pay the appropriate fees when meeting the tree canopy standards of 
Zoning Ordinance 3049 is not feasible on the project site. The Tree 
fund shall be used to plant and maintain new trees in the public right-
of-way in support of Action CS-1.1. 
 
Action CS-1.5 
Update the Landscape Development Manual and Designated Street 
Tree List to prioritize tree species that maximize shade and carbon 
sequestration benefit.

Local Green Economy 
Encouraging residents and visitors to shop locally and supporting local job growth can reduce local and 
global GHG emissions, as well as support the initiatives of the Economic Development Element. Due to 
the complex interaction between local consumer choices and global supply chain emissions the 
measure and actions presented here are not quantified for local GHG reductions in the CAP Update. 
Measure LGE-1 
Sustain local growth through providing green job opportunities to 
residents and promoting local businesses. 
Year 
2030 
2045 
2050 
GHG Reduction 
Potential (MTCO2e) 
Not Quantified 
Not Quantified 
Not Quantified 
With this measure, the City aims to promote sustainable local growth and equity through a 
multifaceted approach. The City plans to take initiatives including partnering with regional 
educational institutions to develop a local workforce equipped with new skills, documenting and 
promoting green practices in local businesses, assisting businesses to apply for sustainability grants, 
and educating the community on environmentally conscious consumption. The City also intends to 
promote local green businesses by educating Oceanside residents and visitors on the 
environmental benefits of buying locally produced goods. The City plans to use its own social media 
accounts and campaigns, including Visit Oceanside and Green Oceanside. Overall, with the focus on 
developing a skilled local workforce for a low carbon economy and collaboration with educational 
institutions, the City intends to enhance local workforce skills, promote local businesses, and 
reduce GHG emissions associated with consumer products. 
Measure LGE-1 Implementing Actions 
Action LGE-1.1 
Partner with the Oceanside Unified School District and MiraCosta 
College and regional workforce organizations to advertise local 
training in trades supporting the low carbon economy, such as electric 
HVAC technologies, renewable energy installation, and EV 
mechanics. 
Action LGE-1.2 
Through a future Sustainable Business recognition program: 
(i)
document innovative sustainable systems practiced by locally
owned businesses,
(ii) promote local green businesses through the City's social media
accounts and publications.

Measure LGE-1 Implementing Actions 
Action LGE-1.3 
Dependent on a dedicated funding source, partner with the Chamber 
of Commerce to develop a program that assists local businesses with 
submitting grant applications for sustainability related initiatives that 
can help expand their operations, such as circular economy initiatives. 
Action LGE-1.4 
Implement an educational campaign highlighting the environmental 
benefits of supporting local businesses, partnering with the Chamber 
of Commerce to maintain a list of businesses that sell locally produced 
goods (e.g., surfboards manufactured in Oceanside, restaurants using 
locally sourced produce). As part of the campaign, work with Visit 
Oceanside and hospitality establishments in the downtown district to 
provide suggestions to visitors for supporting locally produced goods. 
Action LGE-1.5 
Continue educational campaigns through Green Oceanside to provide 
information about sustainable consumption practices and how 
everyday activities, such as grocery shopping and online purchases, 
can be done more sustainably. 
Action LGE-1.6 
Support the continuation and expansion of urban agriculture in the 
City with implementation of HLC policies 7-8 through 7-18.

IMPLEMENTATION AND MONITORING 
5 
5-1 
5 
Implementation and Monitoring 
This chapter outlines the City of Oceanside’s (City’s) approach for implementing the Climate Action 
Plan (CAP) Update and the greenhouse gas (GHG) reduction measures included herein. Successful 
implementation of the CAP Update will require a coordinated effort between City departments, 
stakeholders and the community as a whole. This chapter includes an Implementation and 
Monitoring Matrix where the responsible department(s) and timelines for implementation, as well 
as the key-performance indicators (KPIs) for each GHG reduction measure are clearly identified. 
KPIs are a monitoring tool to understand the effectiveness of the GHG reduction measures in 
reducing communitywide emissions in line with the City’s GHG reduction targets. The City’s 
strategy for monitoring and reporting progress towards the targets is also outlined in this chapter, 
including timelines for both routine reporting and for holistic updates to the CAP. Furthermore, 
the applicability of California Environmental Quality Act (CEQA) streamlining to the CAP Update 
is also discussed in this chapter. 
5.1 
IMPLEMENTATION STRATEGY 
Successful implementation of the CAP Update requires that GHG reduction measures and their 
associated actions are defined with specificity for City staff to understand their role in 
implementation. Each GHG reduction measure includes a suite of actions that are divided amongst 
City departments, with a timeline for implementation provided for each. Additionally, GHG 
reduction measures that have a quantified reduction potential have KPIs that are tied to emissions 
reducing activities. More information about the implementation of individual GHG reduction 
measures and actions is provided at the end of this chapter, including KPIs for GHG reduction 
measures in Table 5-1 and an Implementation and Monitoring Matrix in Table 5-2.  
While individual City departments are assigned certain implementation actions, interdepartmental 
collaboration and communication will be essential to meeting the CAP Update goals. As such, City 
staff will work to develop internal procedures for regular coordination on CAP implementation. 
The initiatives that will be undertaken to coordinate implementation include: 
• 
Hiring or designation of a Sustainability Program Administrator in Fiscal Year 2024-2025 that 
will serve as an interdepartmental coordinator for CAP implementation and collection of data 
for monitoring CAP implementation progress. 
• 
Following CAP Update adoption, quarterly meetings with department leadership to discuss 
CAP implementation status and understand opportunities, challenges and needs of 
departments to support future actions.  
• 
Following CAP Update adoption, providing annual presentations to City Council and relevant 
commissions on CAP implementation status and implementation priorities for the upcoming 
year (Annual Monitoring Reports).  


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
5-2
Regional Collaboration 
Regional collaboration is essential for successful CAP implementation. The City has engaged in 
various regional efforts, including the San Diego Regional Climate Collaborative, SANDAG’s Regional 
Priority Climate Action Plan, the San Diego Regional Climate Action Roadmap, and development of 
the San Diego County Regional Decarbonization Framework. The City will continue to participate in 
and support other regional efforts for climate mitigation and adaptation. Regional engagement 
provides many benefits for the City’s CAP implementation efforts, including: 
•
Helping to build staff capacity by leveraging
regional programs, data and resources;
•
Informing 
local 
decision-making 
by
understanding regional best practices and
funding opportunities;
•
Access to economies of scale that reduce costs
for program implementation for individual
jurisdictions;
•
Enhancing the region’s eligibility for Federal
and State funding; and,
•
Inspiring inter-jurisdictional coordination on
other matters of mutual interest and concern.
Some specific regional collaboration opportunities 
are 
highlighted in the implementation actions for GHG reduction measures; however, more 
opportunities are expected to materialize as the region pursues Federal and State funding for 
programs that can support CAP implementation for individual jurisdictions. 
5.2 
MONITORING AND UPDATES 
The CAP Update is intended to be an 
adaptable, living document that provides 
flexibility for updates as new information 
becomes available and progress is monitored
over time. Monitoring CAP implementation 
requires a two-pronged approach; where 
success is viewed both at the level of a 
measure’s status, and the larger citywide 
GHG emissions trends over time. The City 
will 
begin 
implementing 
CAP 
Update 
measures and actions upon adoption, and 
data tracking will begin the following year. As measures are developed, refined and implemented, 
data on their timing and progress compared with communitywide GHG emissions trends will be 
used to inform updates to the GHG measures and the CAP as a whole. The intent is to allow City 
gO’side Electric Shuttle 
Monitoring and Reporting will consist of: 
• 
Annual progress reports on implementation
status of measures and actions
•
Annual updates to City Council and
Commissions on progress and upcoming 
priorities 
• 
Biennial GHG inventory updates
• 
Updates to the CAP every five years 


 
IMPLEMENTATION AND MONITORING 
5 
5-3 
staff to evaluate and monitor CAP performance over time and alter or amend the plan if it is not 
achieving the desired outcomes. Monitoring actions will include regular tracking of the status of 
each action, analysis of KPIs for reduction measures, and completing regular GHG emissions 
inventory updates. 
Implementation Status Monitoring 
Following adoption of the CAP Update, City staff will annually evaluate the implementation status 
and performance of each GHG reduction measure and its actions. This entails tracking how actions 
are being implemented within their intended timeline and monitoring their progress towards 
expected outcomes. As measures are implemented, the KPI(s) associated with each measure will 
be evaluated to understand if the measure is on track towards achieving the expected community 
benefit and GHG reductions. Through these regular evaluations, the City can identify successful 
measures and actions and reevaluate, adapt and/or replace underperforming measures.  
Greenhouse Gas Emissions Inventory Updates 
GHG emissions inventory updates will be regularly completed such that emissions trends over 
time can be compared for individual sectors and emission sources in the city. During development 
of this CAP Update, the SANDAG Regional Climate Action Planning Framework (ReCAP) provided 
cities with GHG emissions inventories, which informed the City’s 2018 GHG baseline inventory. 
As of 2025, the ReCAP Framework faces an uncertain future to continue providing this service to 
local cities based on funding limitations. As such, the City will work with SANDAG and municipal 
partners in the region to leverage reliable data sources and funding opportunities to update its 
GHG emissions inventories on a biennial cycle to measure progress towards meeting the GHG 
reduction targets. The City will also work with SANDAG to ensure that GHG emissions inventories 
provide “apples-to-apples” comparisons between cycles, consistent with the methods used to 
develop the 2018 baseline GHG emissions inventory. With adoption of this CAP Update the City 
retains a goal of publishing GHG inventory updates every two years.  
Implementation Status Reporting 
Following adoption of this CAP Update, City staff will annually prepare a monitoring report that 
will provide updates on CAP implementation progress, including the status of actions, progress on 
reaching KPIs for reduction measures, and GHG emissions inventory updates every two years. The 
report would serve to provide transparency and promote engagement with the public for CAP 
implementation. The report will also be used to inform annual presentations to the City Council 
and relevant commissions on implementation progress on actions and overall progress towards 
CAP objectives. As technologies and markets change and the City implements the actions in the 
CAP, these reports will be used to track progress and identify measures and actions that require 
adaptation or replacement. 
Finally, the City will prepare a comprehensive update to the CAP every five years, with the next 
comprehensive update occurring by the end of 2030. Updates would reflect the findings and 
recommendations of the monitoring reports and GHG inventory updates. Future updates are 
necessary to account for any new State or Federal legislation, market trends, or regional initiatives 


CLIMATE ACTION PLAN UPDATE 
5-4 
that may change how climate change mitigation is addressed at the local level. The updates also 
provide an opportunity to reassess measures and actions that may have been difficult to 
implement previously due to a lack of available technologies, funding sources or high upfront 
implementation costs. 
Future Measures and Actions for Consideration 
As the City of Oceanside monitors implementation progress of the GHG reduction measures of 
the CAP Update, it may be necessary to update GHG reduction measures to more effectively and 
efficiently meet GHG reduction targets. Throughout the development of this CAP Update, the City 
considered a multitude of reduction measures to reach its GHG reduction targets, with making 
progress towards a 2045 reduction target being the most challenging due to the scale of change 
needed. The measures selected for inclusion in the CAP Update balanced many competing factors 
in what would make for successful and feasible implementation. Two primary factors included the 
availability of resources for the City to develop and implement new policies and programs and the 
costs of these new requirements for residents and businesses. As such, there are potential GHG 
reduction measures that could result in strong GHG reductions that were not included in the CAP 
Update, but may be considered for inclusion if measures are not achieving the projected GHG 
reductions. 
Presented below is a selection of GHG reduction measures that were considered for inclusion in 
the CAP Update but were not seen as feasible due to limited City resources, funding or regulatory 
implications. These measures are being documented here so that the City may consider them for 
future CAP Updates, or if the City is determined to not be on track for meeting GHG reduction 
targets. The City may consider other actions, such as impact fee programs to supplement resources 
to implement the CAP based on funding constraints and other impacts.  
• 
Develop 
a 
not-for-profit 
transportation 
demand management (TDM) agency to assist 
employers 
in 
the 
development 
and 
implementation of TDM plans for commute 
trips reductions. With development of this 
agency, require all existing and new employers 
that generate over 100 vehicle trips per day to 
join the agency, paying a membership fee that 
helps fund local TDM programs that are 
available to members.  
• 
Develop a nonresidential building energy 
benchmarking 
program 
that 
requires 
nonresidential buildings over a certain size to annually report energy consumption to the City 
and reduce energy consumption to below a specified benchmark.  
• 
Implement a Building Energy reach code for new construction that sets a GHG emissions 
efficiency threshold, where buildings must meet a specified GHG emissions level through 
energy efficiency and energy source design features.

5.3 
CALIFORNIA ENVIRONMENTAL QUALITY ACT AND LOCAL 
CLIMATE MITIGATION EFFORTS 
The implementation of the CAP Update will require that new developments are constructed with 
more sustainable, energy-efficient and low-carbon features than existing development. New 
development projects consistent with the growth projections in the City’s General Plan Update 
(incorporated into the CAP Update) and applicable GHG reduction measures are eligible for 
California Environmental Quality Act (CEQA) streamlining of GHG analysis, per the provisions of 
State CEQA Guidelines Section 15183.5. Under these provisions, for a project that is subject to 
discretionary review and is consistent with the City’s General Plan growth projections and can 
demonstrate alignment with the GHG reduction targets of the CAP Update, the level of analysis 
for the project’s GHG emissions required under CEQA can be streamlined. Furthermore, a project’s 
incremental contribution to cumulative GHG emissions may be determined not to be cumulatively 
considerable in such cases.  
The “qualified” CAP will allow project-specific environmental documents, if eligible, to tier from 
and/or incorporate by reference the CAP Update’s programmatic review of GHG impacts in their 
cumulative impact analyses. The CAP Update meets the requirements under Section 15183.5 of 
the State CEQA Guidelines as a qualified plan for the reduction of GHG emissions for use in 
cumulative impact analysis on new development projects. Within one year of CAP Update 
adoption the City will develop and adopt guidelines for discretionary projects subject to CEQA for 
discretionary projects subject to CEQA. The guidelines will provide one or more pathways to 
demonstrate consistency with the CAP Update during the standard development review process.  
The guidelines will be the mechanism used both by private development and future City capital 
projects to demonstrate consistency with the CAP Update. The guidelines will establish a 
threshold of significance and will identify how 
projects can align with the GHG reduction targets 
through various pathways. Consistency with the 
CAP Update may be demonstrated through a CAP 
consistency 
checklist 
that 
documents 
incorporation of all applicable GHG reduction 
measures into a project’s analysis, quantitative 
GHG thresholds, or some combination thereof. 
Measures will be enforced as conditions of 
approval or under existing or future development 
ordinances to ensure that compliance can be 
confirmed before the project can be implemented.

Appendix A 
Greenhouse Gas Emissions 
Forecast and Targets Memorandum


Memo 
 
2550 Fifth Avenue, Suite 640 
 
San Diego, CA 92103 
 
619.219.8000

May 31, 2024, revised October 15, 2025

Justin Gamble (City of Oceanside) 
From: 
Sonam Sahu, Andrew Beecher, Brenda Hom, Poonam Boparai  
Subject: Greenhouse Gas Emissions Forecast and Targets Memorandum for the City of Oceanside Climate 
Action Plan Update  
INTRODUCTION 
The City of Oceanside (City) is currently in the process of updating its Climate Action Plan (CAP), which was first adopted 
in 2019, to provide a programmatic framework for the City to reduce its greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions in alignment 
with State legislation.1 The CAP Update includes a revision of the City’s baseline GHG emissions inventory, a forecast of 
future GHG emissions to align with growth projections of the City’s General Plan Update, and an assessment of the GHG 
emissions reductions that will be needed to meet State GHG reduction targets. This memorandum summarizes the 
results of an updated GHG emissions forecast that aligns with the growth projects of the concurrent General Plan 
Update, and updated GHG reduction targets consistent with Senate Bill 32 (SB 32) and Assembly Bill 1279 (AB 1279).  
The changes to the GHG emissions context for the CAP Update included in this memorandum allow for more 
streamlined monitoring of citywide emissions levels over time. The forecasts also account for the reduction impact of 
State regulations that have been adopted since the City’s 2019 CAP was developed. The 2019 CAP utilized a baseline 
year of 2013 for GHG emission inventory, which is being updated to a 2018 baseline year in the CAP Update. Since 
the 2013 inventory was developed, San Diego Association of Governments (SANDAG) has updated its travel demand 
model utilized for vehicle miles traveled (VMT) modeling and has started providing cities within San Diego county 
with GHG emissions inventories at no cost through the Regional Climate Action Planning Framework (ReCAP) 
Snapshot program. Updating the baseline from 2013 to 2018 allows the City to regularly track GHG emissions with 
consistent data sources and methods through GHG emissions inventories provided by SANDAG. Additionally, several 
State-level regulations have been adopted that provide additional GHG reductions in the City of Oceanside and are 
accounted for in the GHG emissions forecast. These include updated California Building Energy Efficiency Standards 
and the Advanced Clean Cars II regulations. The GHG reduction impact of the City joining the Clean Energy Alliance 
(CEA), to automatically enroll electricity accounts throughout Oceanside at a 50 percent renewable electricity option 
(75% carbon-free electricity equivalent), is also considered as part of the GHG emissions forecast for the CAP Update.  
The purpose of this CAP Update is to develop near- and long-term strategies for reducing GHG emissions in line with 
State targets. As such, the GHG reduction targets of the CAP Update are being adjusted to align with the statewide 2030 
target established by SB 32 and the 2045 target established by AB 1279. Additionally, an updated method for target 
setting is being utilized given the legal precedence established by California Supreme Court decision in Center for 
Biological Diversity v. California Department of Fish and Wildlife and Newhall Land and Farming (2015) 62 Cal.4th 204, and 
the recommendations of the California Association of Environmental Professionals’ Final White Paper Beyond 2020 and 
Newhall (AEP 2016). With this, it is recommended that GHG reduction targets are established on a mass emissions basis (as 
compared to per capita emissions) and that a sector-based approach is taken, where targets are established based on the 
 
1 Assembly Bill 1279 was signed into law in 2022, which formally adopted a long-term statewide target for reducing GHG emissions to net-zero before 2045, 
including an 85% reduction in anthropogenic GHG emissions below 1990 levels before 2045. 


City of Oceanside CAP Update: GHG Emissions Forecasts and Targets 
October 15, 2025 
Page 2 
 
State’s reduction goals for locally specific economic sectors. For example, the State’s targets for reducing emission in the 
“Industrial” economic sector, are not considered for Oceanside’s GHG reduction target, as facilities that fall under this State 
categorization do not exist in the city. This allows for the CAP Update to only consider relevant emissions sources in its 
GHG reduction targets, and provides transparency in how citywide emissions will be reduced in line with State legislation. 
This memorandum summarizes the results of the updated GHG emissions context for the CAP Update, with a 
detailed explanation of the methods and assumptions included in Attachment A GHG Emissions Forecast and Targets 
Technical Documentation. 
ORGANIZATION OF THIS MEMORANDUM 
This memorandum consists of four parts:  
 
Section 1: Greenhouse Gas Emissions Inventory presents an overview of the City’s 2018 communitywide GHG 
emissions inventory. 
 
Section 2: Updated Greenhouse Gas Emissions Forecasts summarizes the forecasted GHG emissions under 
“business-as-usual” (BAU) and Adjusted BAU (ABAU) scenarios for years 2030, 2045, and 2050.  
 
Section 3: Updated Greenhouse Gas Reduction Targets describes the approach for establishing targets for CAP Update.  
 
Section 4: Analysis of Updated Targets Attainment presents the analysis conducted for identifying the emissions 
gap that the City needs to address to achieve the CAP Update targets.  
1 
GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS INVENTORY 
The GHG emissions inventory provides the existing conditions for the CAP Update, serving to identify the contribution of 
different communitywide GHG emissions sources in Oceanside, and establish a baseline for forecasting future emissions, 
setting GHG reduction targets, and monitoring progress towards reaching the target over time. The GHG emissions 
inventory presented here is for calendar year 2018, which was provided by SANDAG through the ReCAP Snapshot program 
(SANDAG 2023).2 An updated GHG emissions inventory is being used to provide a baseline for forecasting future emissions 
from the most recently available data, and a consistent baseline for future emissions inventories. While previous GHG 
inventories have been completed for Oceanside, including a 2013 inventory for the 2019 CAP, use of the updated baseline 
for 2018 allows for consistent comparison of emissions over time with the data sources and methods used by SANDAG. 
Consistent emissions tracking is essential to verify the effectiveness of program and policy implementation for the CAP 
Update. With use of an updated emissions inventory baseline, the GHG emissions forecast, and reduction targets will also 
be updated from those included in the 2019 CAP. The ReCAP Snapshot GHG inventory as provided by SANDAG is included 
as Attachment B to this memorandum. 
Some modifications to the 2018 GHG emissions inventory provided through the ReCAP program have been made to 
provide consistency with GHG emissions forecasting methods and to encompass relevant emissions sources in 
Oceanside. Because the CAP Update is being developed as part of the General Plan Update which includes an 
updated land use scenario, VMT modeling was completed to refine future VMT estimates to be consistent with 
anticipated growth patterns in Oceanside. To align with the VMT modeling completed for the General Plan Update, 
the VMT data used to calculate on-road transportation emissions was updated from that used in the 2018 inventory 
as provided by ReCAP Snapshot. The VMT data provide through ReCAP utilizes the SANDAG ABM2+ model version 
14.0.1; whereas the VMT modeling consistent with the General Plan Update land use scenario uses the SANDAG 
ABM2+ model version 14.3.0, resulting an approximately 2 percent lower VMT total for Oceanside for the year 2018 
than provided through ReCAP. Additionally, the 2018 emissions inventory as provided through ReCAP did not include 
estimates from off-road equipment use, which have been added to the GHG inventory presented here.  
 
2  The GHG emissions inventory provided through ReCAP is a “communitywide” emissions inventory, which covers all relevant GHG emissions sources in the 
City of Oceanside boundary. Municipal government operations are considered within this communitywide inventory, but are not explicitly disaggregated.

1.1 
2018 GHG EMISSIONS INVENTORY RESULTS 
The results of the 2018 communitywide GHG emissions inventory demonstrate the contribution of different GHG 
emissions sources in Oceanside by emissions sector. The largest contributor to total emissions was fuel combustion in 
on-road vehicles, representing 51 percent of total emissions. The next two largest emissions sources were grid-
purchased electricity and natural gas use in buildings and facilities within the city, contributing 21 percent and 19 
percent to total emissions, respectively. The remaining emissions sectors represent approximately 9 percent of total 
emissions, including solid waste, off-road transportation, water, and wastewater. In total, communitywide emissions 
sources considered in the 2018 emissions inventory generated approximately 845,000 metric tons of carbon dioxide 
equivalent (MTCO2e). These results are presented in Figure 1 and Table 1, showing the total emissions by sector with 
the representative contribution to total communitywide emissions.

2 
UPDATED GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS FORECASTS 
The GHG emissions forecast estimates how communitywide GHG emissions for Oceanside may grow given housing 
and economic growth, and how State and Federal regulations may help to reduce local emissions. Developing a GHG 
emissions forecast is an essential step for the CAP, as it will provide insight into what future emissions levels may be, 
and necessary type and scale of actions that will be needed in each GHG emissions sector to reduce emissions within 
local control for State GHG reduction target alignment. As such, a GHG emissions forecast is presented as a sector-
level assessment of near-term (2030) and long-term (2045 and 2050) GHG emissions projections based on current 
conditions under two scenarios. Under the first scenario, GHG emissions would grow from 2018 levels at the same 
rates as housing, population, employment, and vehicle travel, which is known as a business-as-usual scenario (BAU) 
forecast. The BAU forecast serves as a basis for understanding how emissions levels may change with growth, and 
how far GHG emissions will need to be reduced in future years to meet GHG reduction targets without considering 
any future action to reduce GHG emissions. The second scenario considers the GHG reduction impact of local, State, 
and Federal regulations, which is known as a legislative adjusted business-as-usual scenario (ABAU) forecast. The 
ABAU forecast provides a more detailed assessment of future GHG emissions by showing how currently adopted 
local, State and Federal regulations will help Oceanside meet its GHG reduction targets. The future years selected for 
the GHG emissions forecasts are aligned with the GHG reduction target years for State legislation, 2030 for SB 32 and 
2045 for AB 1279, as well as 2050 to align with the General Plan Update horizon year. 


City of Oceanside CAP Update: GHG Emissions Forecasts and Targets 
October 15, 2025 
Page 4 
 
The BAU and ABAU forecasts presented in this memorandum for the CAP Update are updated from those in the 2019 
CAP to incorporate the best available data and to be consistent with the General Plan Update. The updated BAU 
forecast utilizes growth projections that are consistent with the land use scenarios included in the General Plan 
Update and is based on the 2018 GHG emissions inventory, as compared to 2013 in the 2019 CAP. Additionally, the 
ABAU forecast incorporates new State regulations that were not in place during development of the 2019 CAP, such 
as 2022 California Building Energy Efficiency Standards and Advanced Clean Cars II regulations. As such, the CAP 
Update will be based on a more likely future emissions scenario, given an ever-evolving local GHG emission profile 
and the reduction efforts at the State and Federal level. 
Since development of the 2019 CAP, the City of Oceanside has committed to joining the CEA, in which electricity 
accounts in Oceanside was automatically enrolled at the 50 percent renewable electricity / 75% zero-carbon option in 
2024. Since this is a committed local policy that will be officially implemented before the CAP Update is adopted, the 
GHG reduction impact of the City joining CEA is also considered as part of the ABAU forecast.  
2.1 
BUSINESS-AS-USUAL SCENARIO GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS 
FORECAST  
The BAU forecast was developed by scaling communitywide GHG emissions from the 2018 emissions inventory by the 
appropriate growth scaling factors. The growth scaling factors used include population, housing units, and number of 
jobs; and were applied to various emissions sources based on their relationship to the GHG emissions source. For 
example, growth in residential building electricity consumption would be expected to scale with an increase in 
number of housing units, and commercial energy consumption would be expected to scale with the number of 
employees at businesses in Oceanside. More detail on the approach for applying growth scaling factors to specific 
GHG emissions sectors is provided in Attachment A. VMT for on-road transportation is also essential for 
understanding how future GHG emissions may change in Oceanside. VMT was modeled using the SANDAG ABM2+ 
model version 14.3.0, with adjustments made to account for the growth in housing units, population, and jobs 
included in the General Plan Update. The growth scaling factors and VMT used for the BAU forecast were obtained 
from the land use scenario included in the General Plan Update and are provided in Table 2.

2.2 
LEGISLATIVE ADJUSTED BUSINESS-AS-USUAL SCENARIO 
GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS FORECAST  
The ABAU forecast was developed by accounting for the expected GHG reductions in communitywide GHG emissions 
in Oceanside that are expected to occur as a result of adopted local, State, and Federal regulations. The GHG 
reductions considered in the ABAU forecast fall into three categories: building efficiency standards, fuel efficiency 
standards and electric vehicle sale requirements, and renewable electricity requirements. The full list of legislation 
considered in the ABAU forecast is provided in Attachment A. The ABAU scenario also considers the GHG reductions 
that are expected to be realized through the City joining CEA, to provide 50 percent renewable energy / 75% zero-
carbon sources of electricity customers throughout the City starting in 2024.

3 
UPDATED GREENHOUSE GAS REDUCTION TARGETS  
A primary purpose of the CAP Update is to align the City of Oceanside’s GHG reduction targets with the most recent 
legislation. Since the 2019 CAP was adopted, AB 1279 was signed into law, which requires the State to develop and 
implement a strategy for achieving statewide net-zero emissions by 2045 or sooner, which includes an 85 percent 
reduction in anthropogenic GHG emissions by 2045 with remaining emissions offset by carbon dioxide removal. With 
this, the CAP Update will include a long-term GHG reduction target in alignment with AB 1279. The 2030 GHG 
reduction target of the CAP update is maintaining consistency with the 2019 CAP by aligning with SB 32. However, 
the method for setting the 2030 reduction target is being updated to utilize a sector specific approach, which 
considers only the statewide reductions for specific GHG emissions sectors relevant to the City of Oceanside. 
Additionally, a 2050 target is being added to align with the General Plan Update horizon year. The updated targets 
for the CAP Update will provide the City with GHG reduction targets for the years 2030, 2045, and 2050 that utilize 
consistent approaches and align with the most recent State legislation. All GHG reduction targets have been 
translated to be measured from the baseline of the 2018 GHG emissions inventory.  
3.1 
ALIGNING WITH STATEWIDE TARGETS  
The primary statewide GHG reduction targets that are relevant to the CAP Update are those established by SB 32 of 
2016 and AB 1279 of 2022, which include: 
 
Reducing statewide GHG emissions 40 percent below 1990 levels by 2030 (SB 32), and 
 
Reducing statewide anthropogenic GHG emissions 85 percent below 1990 levels before 2045 (AB 1279). 
With the State’s adoption of these legislative targets, the California Air Resources Board was directed to develop and 
implement strategies to reduce statewide emissions consistent with the targets through a Climate Change Scoping 
Plan (Scoping Plan). The Scoping Plan outlines the State’s strategy to reduce GHG emissions in each economic sector 
and is regularly updated to reflect the most recent trends, technology, and legislative reduction targets. The 2017 
Scoping Plan outlines the State’s strategy for achieving the 2030 reduction target of SB 32 (CARB 2017), and the 2022 
Scoping Plan builds upon this strategy to demonstrate how net-zero emissions will be achieved in 2045 or sooner 
consistent with AB 1279 (CARB 2022). Defensible GHG reduction targets at the local level must demonstrate 
alignment with statewide targets and the strategies outlined in the appropriate Scoping Plan. 
For the CAP Update to demonstrate alignment with the State’s legislatively adopted GHG reduction targets, the CAP 
Update must demonstrate an emissions trajectory supported by the State’s Scoping Plan. In the Scoping Plan, GHG 
reduction scenarios are modeled by GHG emission sectors for the State to achieve the corresponding target. The City 
of Oceanside can align GHG reduction targets in the CAP Update with the Scoping Plan’s GHG emission scenarios for 
only the GHG emissions sectors that are relevant to local conditions. For example, the State’s forecasts for reducing 
emission in the “Industrial” sector are not applicable to Oceanside’s GHG reduction target, as facilities that fall under 
this State categorization do not exist in the city. This target setting approach is consistent with the legal precedence 
established by the California Supreme Court decision in Center for Biological Diversity v. California Department of Fish 
and Wildlife and Newhall Land and Farming (2015) 62 Cal.4th 204 and the recommendations of the California 
Association of Environmental Professionals’ Final White Paper Beyond 2020 and Newhall (AEP 2016). More detail on 
the specific sectors relevant to the City of Oceanside is available in Attachment A. 
Basis for the 2030 GHG Reduction Target 
For 2030, the CAP Update’s target is aligned with the SB 32 and the statewide GHG emissions pathways towards 
reaching this target. The 2030 target for the CAP Update is consistent with a 40 percent reduction in statewide GHG 
emissions below 1990 levels, by applying the sector specific targets of relevant GHG emissions sectors to Oceanside’s 


City of Oceanside CAP Update: GHG Emissions Forecasts and Targets 
October 15, 2025 
Page 8 
 
emissions profile as outlined in the 2017 Scoping Plan. This reduction equivalent is translated to be measured from a 
2018 baseline, assuming that GHG emissions in the City have generally followed statewide emissions trends since 1990.  
Basis for the 2045 GHG Reduction Target 
For 2045, the CAP Update’s target is aligned with AB 1279 and the statewide GHG emissions pathways towards 
reaching this target, as outlined in the 2022 Scoping Plan. AB 1279 requires that the State’s net zero emissions target 
by 2045 be achieved by reducing statewide anthropogenic emissions4 by at least 85 percent below 1990 levels before 
2045. The 2045 target for the CAP Update is consistent with an 85 percent reduction in statewide anthropogenic 
GHG emissions below 1990 levels, by applying the sector specific targets of relevant GHG emissions sectors to 
Oceanside’s emissions profile as outlined in the 2022 Scoping Plan. This reduction equivalent is translated to be 
measured from a 2018 baseline, assuming that GHG emissions in the city have generally followed statewide emissions 
trends since 1990.  
Basis for the 2050 GHG Reduction Target 
For 2050, the CAP Update’s GHG reduction target is consistent with the 2045 target, such that GHG emissions cannot 
increase above the 2045 target levels. The purpose for setting a GHG reduction target for the year 2050 is to 
establish an emissions pathway that aligns with the planning horizon of the GPU. Since the State does not specify a 
GHG reduction target beyond that for 2045, the target for the year 2045 is carried forward to 2050.   
3.2 
UPDATED GREENHOUSE GAS REDUCTION TARGETS 
The GHG reduction targets for the CAP Update demonstrate consistency with State legislation through locally specific 
GHG target levels based on the 2018 GHG emissions inventory. The City of Oceanside updated GHG reduction targets 
for the years 2030, 2045, and 2050 are provided in Table 6.

ANALYSIS OF UPDATED TARGETS ATTAINMENT 
The purpose of the CAP Update is to develop strategies the City can implement to reduce communitywide GHG 
emissions consistent with local reduction targets. With the GHG emissions forecast and updated reduction targets 
presented in this memorandum, the City can assess the GHG reductions it will need to achieve to meet the reduction 
targets, or bridge the “emissions gap.” The emissions gap analysis demonstrates the future expected GHG emissions 
levels from the ABAU forecast for each GHG emissions sector, and additional GHG reductions that will need to be 
achieved by the reduction strategies of the CAP Update to align with State GHG reduction targets.  
 
4  Anthropogenic emissions include the primary sources and activities within the City’s GHG emissions sectors: on-road transportation, electricity, natural gas, 
solid waste, off-road transportation, water, and wastewater.

4.2 
MEETING THE TARGETS 
With locally specific GHG reduction targets developed and an understanding of the future emissions gap, the next 
steps are to develop GHG reduction strategies to meet the targets through the CAP Update. The GHG reduction 
strategies of the CAP Update will build upon the successes of the 2019 CAP and provide a pathway for reaching the 
deep GHG reductions needed to align with State GHG reduction targets.

Attachment A 
GHG Emissions Forecast and Targets 
Technical Documentation 


 
City of Oceanside CAP Update 
GHG Emissions Forecast and Targets Technical Appendix 
1 
1 
INTRODUCTION 
This document is a supplement to the Greenhouse Gas Emissions Forecast and Targets Memorandum for the City of 
Oceanside Climate Action Plan Update. This document presents the summary of the 2030, 2045, and 2050 emissions 
projections, explains the projection methodology, and explains the approach used to establish the greenhouse gas 
(GHG) reduction targets for the City of Oceanside (City) Climate Action Plan Update (CAP Update). 
2 
2018 GHG INVENTORY AND ADJUSTMENTS 
The City’s 2018 emissions are directly available from San Diego Association of Governments’ (SANDAG) ReCAP Data 
Portal, which provides the Regional Climate Action Planning Framework (ReCAP) GHG inventory dataset for SANDAG 
jurisdictions (SANDAG 2019). The 2018 inventory from ReCAP consists of emissions from on-road transportation, 
electricity, natural gas, solid waste, water, and wastewater. Two main adjustments are made to this data: the revision 
of the on-road transportation and off-road transportation sectors. 
First, the original ReCAP on-road transportation data are replaced with updated estimates from Chen Ryan Associates 
to be consistent with the modeling approach used for the transportation forecasts for the General Plan update (GPU) 
(Chen Ryan and Associates, pers. comm 2025). Chen Ryan Associates provided on-road vehicle miles traveled (VMT) 
estimates for Oceanside for 2016 and 2050; the interim milestone years (2018, 2030, and 2045) are interpolated from 
these two data points.  
Second, this inventory does not include emissions from off-road transportation. As such, off-road transportation 
emissions are separately added to the city’s 2018 baseline emissions. These emissions are obtained from the 
California Air Resources Board’s (CARB) OFFROAD2021 model. The OFFROAD model estimates air pollutant and GHG 
tailpipe emissions for each county in the state. After running the model for San Diego County, the resulting GHG 
emissions are scaled to the city using different scaling factors depending on the off-road sub-sector, or equipment 
type. The assigned factors are shown in Table 4.  
3 
EMISSIONS FORECAST METHODOLOGY 
The 2018 GHG emissions are forecasted through 2050 based on the city’s growth projections (e.g., population, housing, 
and employment) and known impacts of currently adopted legislation on GHG emissions (e.g., adopted Federal and 
California regulations, policies, and programs affecting fuels, pollutants, and energy efficiency). The following section 
describes the methodology behind forecasting both a “business-as-usual” (BAU) and legislative-adjusted BAU (ABAU) 
emissions from each sector in the CAP Update. The BAU emissions forecast provides an assessment of how emissions 
generated by activities in the city will change over time without further local, State, or Federal action. The ABAU 
emissions forecast includes adopted legislative and regulatory actions at the local, State, and Federal levels that would 
affect emissions without additional action. Both scenarios follow the basic principle of calculating emissions by 
multiplying an activity level (e.g., gallons of gasoline) by its respective emission factor (e.g., grams of CO2 per gallon). 
Additional nuances lie in the calculation of the activity level or the emission factor, which are explained below. 
3.1 
BUSINESS AS USUAL FORECAST 
The city’s BAU forecasted emissions are estimated by scaling the 2018 baseline emissions by three growth factors: 
housing, population, and employment growth in the city. Dyett and Bhatia provided these demographic projections for 
2019 and 2050, which are based on the city’s General Plan Update (Dyett and Bhatia, pers. comm 2025). The CAP 
Update and General Plan Update are scheduled to be completed in tandem and ultimately have a combined CEQA 
review. These demographic projections are used as a basis for the forecasts of activity growth for most sectors.
