3.2 
LEGISLATIVE-ADJUSTED BUSINESS AS USUAL FORECAST 
The ABAU scenario accounts for the effect of adopted legislative and regulatory actions at the local, State, and Federal 
levels on emissions without additional action. This can include both State policy and local programs (e.g., adoption of 
increased renewable electricity through a community choice aggregation program). For building energy, legislative 
reductions affect building energy use through energy efficiency standards and also on building electricity emission 
factors to account for increased zero carbon requirements for the electricity sector. For on-road transportation, off-road 
transportation, and water sectors, legislative reductions affect emissions factors only. Although legislation exists that 
would affect solid waste and wastewater sectors (e.g., SB 1383), there is not sufficient information to quantify the 
legislative reductions from these sectors. However, the impact of such legislation is considered as part of the CAP 
Update’s GHG reduction measures. As such, any additional GHG reductions in these sectors would be factored as part of 
local actions as part of the CAP Update’s GHG reduction measures and not as part of the forecasts. 
Table 5 presents a summary of the legislative adjustments applied to the activity data and emissions factors by sector 
under the ABAU scenario. This is not an exhaustive list, but is a list of the key legislation considered that would result 
in expected local GHG reductions under the ABAU scenario. A more detailed discussion of each sector follows.

3.3 
EMISSION FACTORS BY SECTOR 
3.3.1 On-Road Transportation 
The emissions projections associated with on-road transportation are calculated by multiplying the annual VMT and 
the average vehicle emission factors in Oceanside in 2030, 2045, and 2050. Average weekday VMT for the city is 
provided for 2016 and 2050 by Chen Ryan Associates. The VMT for 2030 and 2045 are linearly interpolated from the 
2016 and 2050 VMT estimates. The total average weekday VMT is multiplied by 347 to convert average weekday VMT 
to average annual VMT, accounting for travel on weekdays and weekends1. The vehicle emission factors are based on 
those from the CARB EMFAC2021 webtool.  
For the BAU forecast, the on-road VMT from Chen Ryan Associates are interpolated between 2016 and 2050 to 
obtain the 2030 and 2045 values. The applied future emission factors (2030 through 2050) are based on 2018 
emission factors from ReCAP, as shown in Table 6. For the ABAU forecast, the future emissions factors are adjusted to 
account for the effects of Advanced Clean Cars II (ACCII) and Advanced Clean Fleets (ACF). The total estimated VMT, 
the average vehicle emission factor, and corresponding GHG emissions from on-road transportation for each 
milestone year are given in Table 6.

The EMFAC2021 model does not include the effects of Advanced Clean Cars II (ACCII) and Advanced Clean Fleets 
(ACF). Therefore, the default EMFAC2021 data was adjusted to account for these regulations for the ABAU forecast, as 
described below. 
 
For ACCII, sales of new light-duty zero-emission vehicles (ZEVs, which comprise both battery electric vehicles and 
plug-in hybrids) were adjusted upwards from the default EMFAC values. ACCII sales requirements for new light-
duty ZEVs increase over time beginning in 2026, reaching 100 percent of new sales in 2035. Data used to 
determine by-year ZEV sales, as well as the split between battery electric vehicles and plug-in hybrids in those 
sales, were derived from CARB (CARB 2022a).  
 
For ACF, sales of medium and heavy-duty trucks were adjusted upwards from EMFAC defaults to meet CARB’s 
target of 100 percent medium- and heavy-duty new vehicle sales by 2036 (CARB 2023)2. The percentage of truck 
sales that were ZEV was assumed to increase linearly year over year to reach 100 percent by 2036. 
Due to these sales mandates, the overall population of ZEVs in the fleet increased relative to EMFAC2021 values. 
Table 7 below summarizes the percentages of ZEVs in the total population, under both the EMFAC2021 scenario and 
the “adjusted” scenario reflecting the regulations described above. The ZEV fleet percentages were used to develop 
citywide vehicle average emission factors that are based on the vehicle-specific emission factors in EMFAC2021. The 
adjusted data was used for the ABAU forecast.

3.3.2 Building Energy 
Emissions associated with the building energy sector are generated from the upstream generation of electricity and on-
site combustion of natural gas. This section presents the methodology behind the forecasted emission factors separately 
from energy use forecasts from non-residential and residential end uses. BAU forecasted energy usage is scaled from 
baseline emissions by the activity growth shown in Tables 2, 3, and 4, but uses the emission factors assumed for 2018 for 
all future years. ABAU forecasts additionally consider the effects of legislation on energy use in new buildings per Title 
24 building energy efficiency standards, as well as changes to the carbon intensity of electricity generation under SB 100, 
RPS, and local energy alliance partnerships that would affect future electricity emission factors. Emissions are calculated 
by multiplying the annual projected building energy use by the respective emission factors. 
EMISSION FACTOR FORECASTS 
Electricity  
San Diego Gas and Electricity (SDG&E) is the main utility that provides electricity gas to the city. In October 2022, the 
City Council pledged to join Clean Energy Alliance (CEA), a community choice aggregation (CCA) program that 
provides renewable electricity options to the city as an alternative power source to SDG&E (The Coast News Group 
 
2  The ACF targets are also adjusted to account for the fact that not all trucks in California are originally sold there (and thus not eligible for the 
sales requirement) (https://ww2.arb.ca.gov/sites/default/files/barcu/regact/2022/acf22/appf.pdf F-14 Table 7). 


Ascent 
 
 
City of Oceanside CAP Update 
GHG Emissions Forecast and Targets Technical Appendix 
7 
2023). Additionally, CEA will begin delivering electricity to accounts in Oceanside starting in 2024. CEA customers are 
automatically enrolled in a default energy service option. CEA customers have the option to opt out of CEA and 
maintain service with SDG&E. The average participation rate for cities served by CEA was 92 percent in 2022 (CalCCA 
2023). It is assumed that from 2024 onward, communitywide electricity accounts will have a similar 92 percent in 
Clean Energy Alliance participation rate with the remaining 8 percent served by SDG&E. 
Under BAU forecasts, SDG&E’s 2018 emissions factor is assumed to remain unchanged through 2050. According to 
ReCAP, SDG&E’s emissions factor in 2018 was 584 lb CO2e per MWh and represented a 43 percent zero-carbon 
electricity mix, meaning that 43 percent of the electricity generated by SDG&E in 2018 was generated by sources that 
emit no GHG emissions. These emission factors are assumed to include transmission and distribution losses, 
according to the information available from ReCAP.  
Under the ABAU forecasts, SDG&E carbon-free mix for 2030, 2045, and 2050 are set to align with the mandates 
outlined in Senate Bill (SB) 1020, Senate Bill 100, and California Public Utilities Commission’s (CPUC’s) Renewables 
Portfolio Standard (RPS) as shown in Table 5. SB 1020 requires that retail electricity sales consist of 60 percent zero-
carbon electricity. SB 100 and RPS require that “eligible renewable energy and zero-carbon resources” supply 60 
percent of retail electricity sales to end-user customers by 2030 and 100 percent by the end of 2045 (CEC 2023). The 
2030 and 2045 emission factors are interpolated from SDG&E’s 2018 emissions factor and the 90 percent and 95 
percent zero-carbon mix by 2035 and 2045 targets, respectively. By 2045, all retail utility emission factors, including 
those for SDG&E, are expected to achieve a 100 percent zero-carbon mix under SB 100.  
CEA’s future planned zero-carbon mix is expected to be more aggressive than State requirements and, subsequently, 
SDG&E’s future resource mix. CEA’s first year of operation was in 2021, during which the CCA had a 50 percent zero 
carbon mix with an emissions factor of 472 lb CO2e per MWh (CEA 2022). CEA expects to provide 81 percent carbon-free 
electricity by 2030 and 100 carbon-free electricity by 2035 (CEA 2023). CEA’s emission factor for 2030 is scaled 
proportionally based on its targeted zero-carbon mix (81 percent). Starting in 2035, CEA’s emission factors would be zero. 
The zero carbon mix of electricity for SDG&E and CEA from 2018 through 2050 are presented in Table 8. The resulting 
electricity emission factors are shown in Table 9.

Natural Gas 
SDG&E is also the main utility that provides natural gas to the city. CEA does not provide natural gas. According to 
ReCAP, 5.45 kilograms of carbon dioxide equivalent is released for every therm of natural gas combusted (kg 
CO2/therm) (SANDAG 2019). Emissions factors associated with natural gas combustion are not anticipated to change 
over time, as there are no legislative actions that would reduce the carbon intensity of natural gas.  
ENERGY USE FORECASTS 
For new buildings, energy use is adjusted to reflect increased stringency under California’s Building Energy Efficiency 
Standards (California Code of Regulations Title 24 Part 6, hereafter referred to as “Title 24”). Title 24 standards apply 
to new construction. The 2019 Title 24 standards apply to projects constructed after January 1, 2020; 2022 Title 24 
standards apply to projects constructed after January 1, 2023; and the next standards will apply after January 1, 2026. 
To estimate adjusted future energy consumption resulting from Title 24 requirements in new construction, electricity- 
and natural gas-specific adjustment factors are calculated using the difference in the average energy use in 
residential and nonresidential buildings between those built to 2019 Title 24 standards and those built to 2022 Title 
24 standards. Adjustment factors are calculated using data available from the California Energy Commission (CEC) 
that were developed for the 2022 Title 24 standards. In addition to accounting for Title 24 requirements by land use 
type (i.e., residential and nonresidential), CEC also developed estimates for energy usage rates by climate zone, and 
the city’s climate zone (Zone 7) is used for the residential buildings analysis. Climate zone-specific data for 
nonresidential buildings are unavailable; therefore, nonresidential adjustment factors relied on statewide averages.  
The adjustment factors (specific to both building type and energy type) are applied to the BAU growth in energy use to 
estimate the energy consumption and associated GHG emissions of future development with legislative adjustments. The 
adjustment factors are shown in Table 11. They are presented in terms of the percent change in energy use for buildings 
compliant with the 2022 Title 24 standards compared to those built to meet the 2019 Title 24 standards. Positive values 
indicate an anticipated increase in energy use, while negative values indicate an anticipated decrease in energy use. It is 
important to note that although average electricity use in new residential buildings is anticipated to rise (due to an 
increase in electrical demand associated with electric appliances installed instead of natural gas appliances), emissions 
from new residential buildings are expected to be lower than they would be under 2019 Title 24 as a result of overall 
lower building emissions intensities (due to lower emissions factors associated with electricity compared to natural gas). 
Table 11 
Title 24 Building Energy Adjustment Factors for 2022 Standards Compared to 2019 Standards 
Building Type 
Electricity 
Natural Gas 
Residential Buildings 
5.6% 
-17% 
Nonresidential Buildings 
-9.5% 
-11% 
Source: Data modeled by Ascent in 2023.

housing and employment, Tables 12 and 13 show the resulting expected energy use by sector and fuel type for years 
2018 through 2050. Note that the CEA participation rate is expected to be constant through 2050 under the ABAU 
scenario. Under the BAU scenario, the 2018 energy usage rates would remain unchanged through 2050.

3.3.3 Solid Waste 
Emissions projections associated with the solid waste sector include emissions from city-generated mixed waste 
through 2050. The solid waste disposal projection through 2050 is based on the service population growth in the city 
and total mixed solid waste disposed in 2018 (124,246 metric tons). No legislative reductions could be applied to this 
sector, so legislative-adjusted BAU emissions are equivalent to BAU emissions. 
Emissions are calculated by multiplying projected annual waste disposed by the mixed waste emissions factor (8.6 kg 
CH4 per MT of waste) and waste diversion rate (67 percent) derived from ReCAP (SANDAG 2019). Key inputs and 
forecasted GHG emissions from solid waste are presented in Table 14.

3.3.4 Off-Road Transportation 
Emissions projections associated with the off-road transportation sector are estimated using CARB’s OFFROAD 
model. CARB released OFFROAD2021, an online emissions inventory database and forecast model for off-road 
equipment and vehicles, in 2021, that generates off-road vehicle emissions by county, vehicle category, equipment 
type, horsepower (HP), and fuel type (CARB 2021). OFFROAD2021 integrates data and legislative-adjusted forecasts 
from several updated off-road models, such as Small Off-Road Equipment 2020, which generates emissions for off-
road vehicles with engines less than or equal to 25 HP, RV 2018, which generates emissions for recreational vehicles, 
and other sector-specific models. Applicable legislative reductions include EPA’s off-road compression-ignition 
engine standards implementation schedule (e.g., diesel engine tier requirements) and CARB’s Off-Road Diesel 
Regulation. Although these legislative reductions focus on mitigating criteria air pollutants, they also result in 
increased efficiency which can lead to reduced fuel use and GHG emissions.  
Specific data on off-road vehicles for the city were not available, therefore, the emissions estimated in each sub-sector for 
San Diego County are allocated to the city based on various scaling factors according to each sub-sector. Future growth in 
off-road equipment activity is taken directly from OFFROAD2021 outputs for San Diego County and scaled down to the 
city. Activity growth could not be separated from impacts from legislation. Thus, the BAU and ABAU forecasts are the same 
for this sector, and both account for legislative reductions. Table 15 presents the scaling factors for each sub-sector. Off-
road fuel consumption estimates for the city are shown in Table 16 and the resulting GHG emissions are shown in Table 17.

3.3.5 Water 
Emissions from the water sector are generated by the consumption of electricity for processes such as pumping, 
treatment, and conveyance of water. Emissions projections associated with the water sector include emissions from 
electricity embedded in the water supply for water consumed in the city through 2050. The water consumption 
projections through 2050 are based on the service population3 growth in the city and the total water consumed in 
2018. Under the BAU scenario, emission factors are expected to remain unchanged into the future from the 2018 
emissions factors available from ReCAP (SANDAG 2019). Under the ABAU scenario, emissions factors for both potable 
water and recycled water account for an increasing percentage of zero-carbon electricity based on legislative

3.3.6 Wastewater 
Emissions projections associated with the wastewater sector account for emissions generated during the treatment of 
wastewater. These consists of emissions from combustion of methane produced (e.g., combustion of generated 
digester gas) and fugitive methane and nitrous oxide (N2O) emissions. The wastewater production through 2050 is 
assumed to be proportional to population growth in the city. No legislative reductions could be applied to this sector, 
so legislative-adjusted BAU emissions are equivalent to BAU emissions. Wastewater-related emission factors are 
assumed to remain unchanged into the future. Therefore, wastewater emissions are forecasted by directly scaling 
2018 wastewater emissions by the city’s population. Table 20 presents results from the wastewater sector.

4 
GREENHOUSE GAS REDUCTION TARGETS AND GAP 
ANALYSIS 
The target setting process for the CAP Update involves identifying the appropriate State and regional adopted GHG 
reduction targets and then relating these targets to Oceanside-specific emissions levels based on Oceanside’s 
emissions generating activities and jurisdictional control for affecting these emissions. Reduction percentages for the 
city that are in alignment with the statewide and regional reduction targets are developed to provide mass emissions 
and percent reduction targets based on the most recent GHG emissions inventory for the 2018 year. The following 
section provides detail on how the GHG reduction targets for the CAP Update were determined based on consistency 
with statewide GHG emissions trends and the State’s GHG emissions inventory.  
4.1 
ALIGNING WITH STATEWIDE TARGETS 
The first step in the target-setting process is to identify the relevant State laws and plans with which the CAP Update’s 
GHG reductions should be in alignment. The CAP Update’s GHG reduction targets and goals are informed by the 
following State plans and laws:  
 
Reducing emissions to 48 percent below 1990 levels by 2030 (per the 2022 Scoping Plan), 
 
Reducing emissions to 85 percent below 1990 levels by 2045 (per AB 1279). 
4.1.1 Statewide Sectors Applicable to the City of Oceanside 
In order to develop local GHG reduction targets that are consistent with the State’s GHG emissions inventory and 
projections modeling, an assessment was conducted to demonstrate the applicability of the State inventory GHG 
emissions sectors to Oceanside’s GHG emissions profile. A review of the 2022 Scoping Plan demonstrates that the City 
has direct or indirect jurisdiction over four of the eight emissions sectors included in the statewide emissions inventory: 
residential and commercial, electric power, recycling and waste, and transportation. This review is summarized in Table 21.  
Table 211 
2022 Scoping Plan Emissions Sectors Applicable to the City of Oceanside 
Emissions Sectors – 2022 Scoping Plan 
Applicable to City of Oceanside? 
Agriculture 
No 
Residential and Commercial 
Yes 
Electric Power 
Yes 
High Global Warming Potential (GWP) Gases 
No 
Industrial 
No 
Recycling and Waste 
Yes 
Transportation 
Yes 
Carbon Dioxide Removal (CDR) 
No 
Source: Data modeled by Ascent in 2023. 
The agriculture, high global warming potential (GWP) gases, industrial, and CDR sectors are excluded for the 
following reasons. First, the City has small agricultural parcels with marginal emissions associated with livestock 
activities under its jurisdiction; whereas the State’s agricultural GHG emissions are largely dominated by livestock 
emissions. Secondly, the City has limited to no ability to control or influence emissions of high GWP gases because it 
has limited or no jurisdiction or influence over the following activities in the city: substitution of ozone-depleting 
substances with high GWP gas substitutes; emissions of sulfur hexafluoride (SF6) from electricity transmission lines; 
and semiconductor manufacturing processes. Thirdly, the City does not have jurisdiction to affect GHG emissions 
sources from industrial facilities that are covered entities under the State’s Cap-and-Trade program. Lastly, the State’s 


 
 
Ascent 
 
City of Oceanside CAP Update 
14 
GHG Emissions Forecast and Targets Technical Appendix 
CDR sector identifies significant reductions from engineered strategies to remove significant levels of emissions from 
the atmosphere using technologies like direct air capture and CCS. Constructing and operating direct air capture 
machines to remove GHG emissions from the atmosphere is outside the scope of local governments in California, 
including the City. In addition, the city does not include large-scale petroleum refineries, GHG-emitting electric power 
plants, cement manufacturing facilities, or other large-scale industrial facilities that could have their GHG emissions 
reduced using CCS technologies. To achieve statewide net zero emissions by 2045, the 2022 Scoping Plan relies on 
large-scale deployment of CCS technologies and CDR strategies. However, the City government does not have the 
jurisdiction or other ability to construct and operate CCS and mechanical CDR strategies. 
By excluding these sectors under this approach, community GHG reduction targets for the City can be established in 
proportion with statewide reductions for all sectors relevant to City jurisdiction to the extent feasible using available 
data. This target-setting approach is consistent with the California Supreme Court decision in Center for Biological 
Diversity v. California Department of Fish and Wildlife and Newhall Land and Farming (2015) 62 Cal.4th 204, which 
determined that the approach of assessing a project’s consistency with statewide emissions reduction goals must 
include a “reasoned explanation based on substantial evidence” that links the project’s emissions (in this case, the 
project is the CAP, which covers communitywide emission) to statewide GHG reduction goals.  
Note that CAP Update sectors are tailored to local needs and focus on specific priorities. Hence these sectors stem 
from the 2022 Scoping Plan sectors but are not identical to Scoping Plan sectors. For example, the Residential and 
Commercial sector from the Scoping Plan only refer to on-site emissions from these sources (primarily natural gas 
combustion for building end uses). Electricity-related emissions (including electricity consumption for water) are 
addressed in the Electric Power sector in the Scoping Plan.  
4.1.2 Target Emissions Reduction Levels and Percentages for 
Oceanside 
The next step is to translate the statewide target percentages to the city. CARB estimates the targeted future 
emissions levels for each year from 2020 to 2045 for each sector (CARB 2022c). The analysis uses 2018 data from the 
State’s emission inventory and sector-level forecasted reduced emissions in 2030 and 2045 from the 2022 Scoping 
Plan (CARB 2022b and 2022c). These forecasted emissions are aligned with the State’s target to reach 48 percent 
below statewide 1990 levels in 2030 and 85 percent below 1990 levels in 2045. In order to translate these targets to 
be relative to 2018, statewide emissions in future years from the applicable sectors are compared to 2018 statewide 
emissions from applicable sectors to determine the percentage reduction for the city. Data for 2018 are used because 
1990 emissions data are not available for the city and because 2018 is the baseline year of the GHG emissions 
inventory used for the City’s CAP Update. This analysis is summarized in Table 22.

1) Update the OFFROAD2021 tab with latest run for jurisidiction in question. 
2) Enter the jurisdiction scaling method for each sub-sector. Choose from the drop down cell. For sub-sectors that are not active in the jurisdiction, leave the jurisdiction scaling method blank.
3) Emissions will automatically be calculated from the OFFROAD fuel outputs based on the jurisdiction scaling method chosen. 
4) Do not add or remove any rows that are already in the table below. The rows have been prepopulated with all possible sub-sectors and fuel types. Only adjust the jurisdiction scaling method and leave blank, if not 
applicable.
• Enter Inventory Data into the colored cells starting in row 4. Grey cells are for calculations only.
• Insert rows within the table as needed. 
• Only input data in the colored cells.
• See comments in headers for specific input instructions.

Subject: GHG Reduction Measures Technical Memorandum for the City of Oceanside Climate Action Plan Update 
INTRODUCTION 
The City of Oceanside (City) is currently in the process of updating its Climate Action Plan (CAP), which was first adopted 
in 2019, to provide a programmatic framework for the City to reduce its greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions in alignment 
with State legislation.1 The CAP Update includes the development of GHG reduction measures that will set the City on a 
pathway towards alignment with the GHG reduction targets established by Senate Bill 32 (SB 32) and Assembly Bill 1279 
(AB 1279). An updated GHG emissions forecast has been completed, which estimates how future emissions may evolve 
as growth in the City continues for the years 2030, 2045, and 2050, as provided in the Greenhouse Gas Emissions 
Forecast and Targets Memorandum for the City of Oceanside Climate Action Plan Update (GHG Forecast and Targets 
Memo), which is included as part of the CAP Update as Appendix A Greenhouse Gas Emissions Forecast and Targets 
Memorandum. The GHG emissions forecast presents two future scenarios, a business-as-usual scenario (BAU) and a 
legislative adjusted business-as-usual scenario (ABAU). The BAU forecast demonstrates how GHG emissions in the City 
would increase with growth in housing units, population, and jobs; while the ABAU forecast demonstrates how future 
GHG emissions in the BAU forecast would be expected to change when accounting for the GHG reduction impact of 
State, Federal, and local legislation and programs. The GHG Forecast and Targets Memo also outlines GHG reduction 
targets for the CAP Update that would align with the State legislative targets under SB 32 and AB 1279. Comparing the 
results of the ABAU forecast with the GHG reduction targets shows that the City would need to implement additional 
policies and programs to further reduce GHG emissions in Oceanside to align with State legislative targets in the CAP 
Update.  
A suite of GHG reduction measures have been developed for inclusion in the CAP Update that can be feasibly 
implemented by the City to reduce GHG emissions in alignment with State legislative targets. Each GHG reduction 
measure is categorized under a GHG emissions sector for which the measure’s implementation would reduce GHG 
emissions. These “sectors” do not always directly align with those presented in the City’s GHG emissions inventory, as 
some measures are cross-cutting in nature, while others are intended to provide additional community benefits while 
reducing emissions from sources not accounted for in the City’s GHG emissions inventory.2 Each GHG reduction measure 
also includes implementing actions that provide discrete steps for the City to take in successfully meeting the goals of 
 
1 Assembly Bill 1279 was signed into law in 2022, which formally adopted a long-term statewide target for reducing GHG emissions to net-zero before 2045, 
including an 85% reduction in anthropogenic GHG emissions below 1990 levels before 2045. 
2  The primary sectors by which measures are organized that do not align with the City’s GHG emissions inventory sectors are Carbon Sequestration and Local 
Green Economy. Carbon Sequestration is not accounted for in the City’s current inventory, as a comprehensive study on the amount of carbon sequestered in 
natural and agricultural lands and the urban forest is not currently available for Oceanside. The Local Green Economy sectors is also not accounted for, as it is 
intended to reduce emissions associated with the consumption of consumer products, by which GHG emissions are emitted outside of Oceanside’s boundary 
or control across the globe throughout product supply chains. 


Oceanside CAP Update GHG Reductions Memo 
May 31, 2024, revised October 28, 2025 
Page 2 
 
measures. These “actions” have been developed to outline sufficient detail for both stakeholders and City departments 
tasked with implementation to have transparency in the process of the City’s transition towards a low carbon future.  
This memorandum presents the GHG reduction measures and implementing actions for consideration in the City’s 
CAP update, with the quantified GHG reduction potential for each measure. The measures and actions have been 
developed in coordination with City staff and are based on measures identified in the 2019 CAP, the General Plan 
Update (GPU), as well as new measures and actions informed by current best practices and responses to public 
comments. The measures and actions strive to balance the need for deep GHG reductions to meet State aligned 
targets, the availability of resources for the City to implement new programs, and equity considerations. Details on 
the calculation of GHG reductions associated with each measure are provided in Attachment A GHG Reduction 
Potential Technical Documentation. 
ORGANIZATION OF THIS MEMORANDUM 
This memorandum consists of four parts:  
 
Section 1: Greenhouse Gas Emissions Forecast, Targets, and Gap Analysis Summary describes the results of the 
ABAU forecast, recommended GHG reduction targets, and the “emissions gap” that would need to be met with 
GHG reduction measures in the CAP Update, as detailed in the Forecast and Targets Memo. 
 
Section 2: Greenhouse Gas Reduction Measures Summary provides an overview of the GHG reduction measures 
considered for the CAP Update and their calculated GHG reduction potential.  
 
Section 3: Greenhouse Gas Reduction Measures Detail outlines each GHG reduction measure, and the 
implementing actions associated with each measure.  
GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSIONS FORECAST, TARGETS,  
AND GAP ANALYSIS SUMMARY 
The results of the ABAU GHG emissions forecast, reduction targets, and gap analysis, as presented in the Forecast 
and Targets Memo, are summarized here to provide the GHG emissions context for the reduction measures 
considered for the CAP Update. The ABAU forecast shows how emissions are expected to change in the City of 
Oceanside for the years 2030, 2045, and 2050, considering the growth projections of the in-progress General Plan 
Update, and the impacts of State and Federal legislation on local GHG emissions. The impact of the City joining the 
Clean Energy Alliance is also considered in the ABAU forecast. The GHG reduction targets are aligned with SB 32 and 
AB 1279 statewide GHG reduction targets, which include: 
 
Reducing statewide GHG emissions 40 percent below 1990 levels by 2030 (SB 32), and 
 
Reducing statewide anthropogenic GHG emissions 85 percent below 1990 levels no later than 2045 (AB 1279). 
These statewide targets were converted into local targets for the CAP Update by adjusting the baseline from 1990 to 
2018 and reduction percentages based on the State’s sector-level analysis of past and future GHG emissions. The gap 
analysis is done by comparing the results of the ABAU forecast and the local targets for the CAP Update to understand 
the magnitude of emissions reductions that will need to be achieved through measures in the CAP Update. This 
“emissions gap” is key for determining the actions that will be needed to address GHG emissions in each sector. 
The ABAU forecast, reduction targets, and gap analysis are summarized in Table 1. The results show that GHG 
emissions are expected to decrease below 2018 levels through 2045; however, an emissions gap still remains between 
emissions reduction under the ABAU scenario and future emissions levels consistent with State legislative reduction 
targets. Based on the results of the ABAU forecast, GHG emissions reduction measures can be targeted towards the 


Oceanside CAP Update GHG Reductions Memo 
May 31, 2024, revised October 28, 2025 
Page 3 
 
GHG emissions sectors that contribute most to citywide emissions, including natural gas, waste, on-road 
transportation, and off-road transportation.

GREENHOUSE GAS REDUCTION MEASURES SUMMARY 
The GHG reduction measures presented below are organized under six focus sectors that generally align with 
emissions sectors considered in the 2018 GHG emissions inventory, including: 
 
 Energy, which includes measures that reduce emissions in the natural gas and electricity GHG inventory sectors;  
 
Transportation, which includes measures that reduce emissions in the on-road transportation and off-road 
transportation GHG inventory sectors; 
 
Solid Waste;  
 
Water;  
 
Carbon Sequestration, which reduces the City’s overall GHG emissions contribution; and  
 
Local Green Economy, which is intended to reduce upstream emissions not accounted for in the GHG inventory.  
Each sector includes one or more GHG reduction measures, and each measure includes multiple implementation 
actions. Implementation actions include one or more key actions that drive quantifiable GHG reductions, as well as 
multiple supportive actions that support effective implementation of the measures and key actions. 
Performance indicators are provided for measures that have quantifiable GHG reductions to allow the City to monitor 
progress towards meeting the GHG reduction goal of the measure as the actions of the measure are implemented. 


Oceanside CAP Update GHG Reductions Memo 
May 31, 2024, revised October 28, 2025 
Page 4 
 
Implementation progress for reduction measures that do not include performance indicators can be monitored 
based on whether the implementation actions have been completed.  
GHG reductions associated with the measures were calculated in a stepwise manner for the future years 2030, 2045, 
and 2050. In other words, GHG reductions (in MTCO2e) are assessed during a snapshot in time in years 2030, 2045, 
and 2050. Measures are quantified for a single year rather than adding cumulative reductions from prior years, which 
aligns with the methodology used to develop the City’s GHG reduction targets. Importantly, GHG emissions 
reductions were quantified for measures wherever substantial evidence and reasonable assumptions were available 
to support calculations. Some of the GHG reduction measures were identified as not quantifiable because of lack of 
available data or quantification methods but would still be expected to reduce GHG emissions. These measures are 
listed in this technical memorandum and will be discussed qualitatively in the CAP Update. They can be monitored for 
potential quantification opportunities in the future if data and/or quantification methods become available.  
Estimates of GHG emissions reductions, along with an estimated emissions “gap” (i.e., the difference between GHG 
reductions required to meet the targets and total estimated GHG reductions), are summarized in Table 2. 
Descriptions of the measures are provided in the following sections. Detailed measure calculations and assumptions 
supporting the GHG reduction estimates are provided in Attachment A.

GREENHOUSE GAS REDUCTION MEASURES DETAIL 
The following section provides details on the intent and structure of each GHG reduction measure for consideration 
in the CAP Update, as well as additional information about the quantification of GHG reductions associated with the 
measure. Each measure includes a measure summary, which provides an overview of how the implementing actions 
support GHG reductions and successful implementation of the measure. A brief description of the approach for 
quantifying GHG reductions with the measure is also provided, highlighting key data and assumptions, with further 
detail on calculations provided in Attachment A. Lastly, the full set of implementation actions for each GHG reduction 
measure is provided in a table, with the quantified GHG reductions. The actions are noted as “key” or “supportive” to 
highlight which actions are intended to create effective change in activities that generate GHG emissions and which 
actions are supportive to the key actions and the overall successful implementation of the measure. Additionally, 
actions that support equity and the allocation of the benefits of programs to all residents are noted as “inclusive.”  
ENERGY 
GHG emissions from energy consumption and generation represented 40% of the City’s total emissions in 2018. Even 
as electricity-related emissions are reduced through procurement of renewable electricity from joining Clean Energy 
Alliance, natural gas consumption will continue to represent a significant proportion of the City’s total GHG emissions 
through 2045 and 2050. The GHG reduction measures and actions included in the Energy sector strive to promote 
energy efficiency, increase renewable energy availability, and reduce future consumption of natural gas. 
Measure E-1: Provide 100% renewable/zero-carbon energy delivery through the Clean Energy 
Alliance (CEA) by 2030 with a 95% participation rate. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
To reduce emissions associated with electricity, the City plans to increase the availability of renewable energy to 
community members. The City has already made significant progress by joining the Clean Energy Alliance (CEA), a 
community choice aggregation (CCA) program, providing residents and businesses with 50% renewable electricity as 
a default for those who do not opt-out as of January 2024. The City can further the impact of joining CEA by 
purchasing 100% renewable electricity and increasing the default electricity rate to 100% renewable electricity for 
accounts in the community. Regular outreach campaigns will be conducted to encourage continued participation in 
CEA, which includes sharing information online and promoting CEA participation at community events.  
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
The quantification of GHG reductions for Measure E-1 assumes a reduction in the emissions intensity of the electricity 
supply for Oceanside. Calculations assumed 95% of all electricity consumption in the City is 100% renewable/zero-
carbon in 2030, resulting in GHG reductions beyond the statewide minimum Renewables Portfolio Standard (RPS) 
requirements for GHG-free electricity. In 2045 and 2050, no additional reductions are seen from implementation of 
this measure, as all retail electricity generation in the state is expected to be 100% GHG-free, consistent with RPS 
minimum requirements. Double counting of emissions reductions from other energy related measures was avoided 
by accounting for the reduction in grid-supplied electricity from increased solar photovoltaic (PV) om Measures E-2 
and increased energy efficiency in Measure E-3 prior to accounting for the shift to more GHG-free electricity.

Action E-1.1

by 2025 at the time of enrollment. (Completed) 
Action E-1.2 
Enroll 100% of City municipal operations in 100% renewable/zero-carbon option from CEA by 2030. (Key) 
Action E-1.3 
Update the agreement with CEA to increase the default for all CEA customers in the community to 100% renewable/zero-
carbon energy by 2030. (Key) 
Action E-1.4 
Annually track CEA participation rates, including opt-out and opt-down trends, and work with CEA to develop strategies for 
maintaining or increasing participation. (Supportive) 
Action E-1.5 
Conduct annual community engagement campaigns to promote CEA renewable energy choices and programs through print, 
social media and digital media through sustainability campaigns and community events. (Supportive) 
Measure E-2: Increase behind-the-meter solar photovoltaic (PV) capacity in the City to 125 
megawatts (MW) by 2030 and 165 MW by 2045. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
To enhance the resilience of the city's energy infrastructure and reduce reliance on grid supplied electricity, the City plans 
to increase the availability of local renewable energy in Oceanside. Through incremental expansion of the PV capacity at 
both City and privately-owned properties, the City plans to achieve at least 125 MW of communitywide solar capacity by 
2030 and 165 MW by 2045. The City will ensure installation of solar infrastructure through adopting building code 
standards that require solar on all new construction and installing solar arrays at City-owned facilities. This may be 
achieved through adoption of the 2025 Building Energy Efficiency Standards or a reach code designed to exceed those 
standards. The City will also support the voluntary installation of solar infrastructure through expediting the permitting 
process, promoting financing options, and partnering with CEA to support installation of solar at low-income households.  
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
The quantification of GHG reductions for Measure E-2 is based on the shift of grid supplied electricity to GHG-free 
energy generated on-site through solar PV. The reduction quantification assumes that the targets of 125 MW citywide 
solar PV capacity by 2030 and 165 MW capacity by 2045 can feasibly be met through building code standards and 
direct installation by the City, beyond the 78 MW capacity as of 2020 (SANDAG 2023). The additional installation does 
not account for voluntary installation of solar on private property. Recent changes in the cost-effectiveness of solar 
installation due to regulatory changes in the net-metering rate structure for solar PV make it difficult to estimate 
future voluntary installations based on past trends. Double counting of emissions reductions with Measure E-1 was 
avoided by accounting for the grid demand reduction from Measure E-2 through shifting to onsite renewables, prior 
to adjusting for GHG-free grid supplied electricity.

Measure E-3: Launch an “Energy Upgrade Oceanside Program” with a goal of achieving 400 
building energy efficiency retrofits by 2030 and 60,000 by 2045 with primary end uses 
converted to electric. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
To reduce emissions in existing buildings and support more efficient homes and businesses, the City will develop and 
launch an “Energy Upgrade Oceanside Program” to strategically transition Oceanside towards more efficient and 
primarily electricity-fueled buildings. Initial steps would include adoption of a “reach code” to require retrofits and 
renovations of specified scope to implement cost-effective efficiency upgrades to meet an efficiency level based on a 
scoring criterion. Reach codes are amendments to the City’s local building codes that exceed the minimum 
requirements of the California State Building Energy Efficiency Standards (Title 24, Part 6) that are subject to approval 
by the California Energy Commission. The implementation of these upgrades will be supported through additional 
funding opportunities and sharing information on cost-saving opportunities such as tax credits and rebates. Longer-
term decarbonization of buildings will require broader strategies and mechanisms to support beneficial electrification 
of existing buildings, not increasing costs to residents and businesses. As such the City will develop a detailed existing 
building decarbonization strategy that analyzes the specific challenges and opportunities for electrification in 
Oceanside and develops a phased long-term strategy for retrofitting the majority of buildings in the city. As part of 
the Energy Upgrade Oceanside Program, the City will also retrofit its own buildings and facilities to improve efficiency 
and switch to electric end-uses where feasible and cost effective.  
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
The quantification of GHG reductions for Measure E-3 is based on energy efficiency and electrification measures 
applied to residential and non-residential buildings in the City. The reduction quantification assumes that efficiency 
upgrades are performed at time-of-renovation through the requirements of an existing buildings reach code up to 
the year 2030, affecting 400 permitted building retrofits, including both residential and non-residential buildings, or 
400 per year starting in 2027. This value is estimated based on the number of retrofits that were required to comply 
with the City of Carlsbad’s existing building energy efficiency reach codes in 2022, which was 108, assuming that the 
reach code triggers and rate of communitywide retrofits are generally similar to Oceanside (Carlsbad 2023). After 
2030, it is assumed that a longer-term strategy would be developed to retrofit approximately 90% of existing 
buildings in Oceanside, which is roughly estimated at 60,000 buildings based on the number of existing housing units 
and average non-residential building square footage estimates. The GHG reductions from energy efficiency increases 
are primarily associated with switching fuels from natural gas to electric for primary end uses of heating, ventilation, 
and air conditioning (HVAC) and domestic hot water heating. Efficiency increases were modeled based on a reach 
code option representative of the reach code presented in Action E-3.1.

May 31, 2024, revised October 28, 2025

Action E-3.1 
Develop an “Existing Building Energy Conservation Ordinance” requiring existing buildings with qualifying improvements 
(addition of 750 square feet [sf] for residential, 1,500 sf or 15% above existing sf for non-residential) to concurrently 
implement energy efficient upgrades. Provide options to select and implement efficiency measures from a prescriptive list, 
which collectively achieve an efficiency level based on a scoring criterion. The analysis should identify which efficiency 
measures are considered cost effective for different building use types. (Key) 
Action E-3.2 
Adopt the “Existing Building Conservation Ordinance” consistent with the results of a cost-effectiveness analysis in Action 
E-3.1 by 2027. (Key)
Action E-3.3 
Develop a tracking system for the types of energy efficiency upgrades completed by applicants from the “Existing Building 
Conservation Ordinance” within the existing permit database software, including building primary use, annual energy 
consumption, square footage of retrofits, types of upgrades completed, and end uses electrified. (Supportive) 
Action E-3.4 
Every three years, following adoption of the “Existing Building Conservation Ordinance,” review the ordinance’s 
effectiveness in encouraging residents to perform cost-positive energy conservation measures and increase the stringency 
of the program or adjust the threshold of qualifying improvements as needed to reduce communitywide energy 
consumption. Reviews and updates to the ordinance will be coordinated to align with the triennial State Building 
Standards Code update cycle. (Supportive) 
Action E-3.5 
Support proposed GP policy HLC 7-142 by establishing a revolving loan fund to provide low-interest loans to low- and 
fixed-income residents to cover the costs of time-of-replacement electrification upgrades, which may include replacement 
of hot-water heaters and heating, ventilation, and air conditioning (HVAC) units with electric heat pump options. Provide 
that the loans can be processed in reduced timeframes to the extent feasible to enable replacement of equipment in a 
timely manner and that funds cover the cost of electric equipment replacements, including panel upgrades if needed. Use 
development mitigation fees, CEA residual revenue, U.S. Department of Housing and Development’s (HUD's) Green and 
Resilient Retrofit Program (GRRP)'s Grants and Loans component and other sources for capitalizing the revolving loan 
fund. Implement a targeted outreach campaign with loan establishment that allows residents ready access to program 
information (e.g., magnets or mailers). (Inclusive) 
Action E-3.6 
Launch a Sustainable Business recognition program to provide education on energy efficiency upgrade funding options, 
including SDG&E rebates and incentives, SDG&E on-bill financing for commercial uses, 179D tax credits for commercial 
uses, and any CEA programs as available. This should include, but not be limited to, virtual and in-person seminars, emails, 
the City's website, and during the Sustainable Business network application process. (Supportive) 
Action E-3.7 
Implement the recommendations included in the Opterra 2023 “Energy Conservation Measures Analysis” completed for 
municipal facilities for efficiency and electrification measures at City facilities, by including projects in the City's Capital 
Improvement Plan based on funding and grant availability. (Key) 
Action E-3.8 
By 2030, develop an Existing Building Decarbonization Strategy that analyzes the specific challenges and opportunities for 
electrification in Oceanside and develops a phased long-term strategy for retrofitting the majority of buildings in the City 
without increasing costs for residents and businesses. (Key) 
Measure E-4: Require that all new construction be electric ready by 2026. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
To reduce dependence on fossil fuels in the future, the City plans to adopt the 2025 Building Energy Efficiency 
Standards to promote electrification and ensure new buildings are prepared for electric infrastructure. This will reduce 
building energy-related emissions and prepare the city for future electrification efforts. Through this measure, the 
City intends to make the transition to electric power easier for the community and ensure long-term cost savings for 
residents and businesses. The City plans to respond to the increased demand for contractors, technicians, and 


Oceanside CAP Update GHG Reductions Memo 
May 31, 2024, revised October 28, 2025 
Page 10 
construction workers with expertise in electrical systems by partnering with regional organizations and providing 
training to the local workforce through both Action E-4.2 and additional actions under Measure LGE-1. Additionally, 
all newly constructed City facilities will be all electric, starting in 2026. 
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
The quantification of GHG reductions for Measure E-4 is based on the expected changes in the California Building 
Energy Efficiency Standards between the 2022 and 2025 code cycles, which is expected to require that all HVAC and 
water heating end uses are electric heat pumps. The quantification accounts for the switch from natural gas to 
electricity for single family units and mid-rise multi-family units that do not already require electric end uses in the 
2022 code, which was used to model future building emissions in the ABAU forecast. The quantification assumes that 
the standard would be adopted in 2025 with compliance required for all new buildings beginning 2026.

TRANSPORTATION 
Transportation was the largest GHG emissions source in the City in 2018, representing 52% of total emissions, and will 
continue to be the largest GHG emissions source through 2045 and 2050. The key lever the City has to reduce 
transportation-related emissions is through a reduction in vehicle miles traveled (VMT) and facilitating the increased 
adoption of zero emission vehicles and equipment, which are the focus of the measures and actions in the 
Transportation sector. 
Measure TR-1: Reduce per-capita vehicle miles traveled (VMT) 3% by 2030 and 6% by 2045 
from 2016 levels through transportation demand management (TDM) strategies. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
To reduce emissions associated with vehicular travel, the City plans to require and support TDM strategies to reduce 
VMT through multiple actions. A primary action is to amend the City’s current TDM Ordinance to increase the 
number of businesses that are required to comply with trip reduction requirements by capturing redevelopment 
projects and sites where a conditional use permit is being issued or updated. Additionally, the City will continue to 
implement VMT significance thresholds for projects subject to California Environmental Quality Act (CEQA) review 
and SB 734 analysis such that projects do not increase per capita VMT. The City will also continue to implement and 
expand micro-transit options and support City employee commute trip reductions. 


Oceanside CAP Update GHG Reductions Memo 
May 31, 2024, revised October 28, 2025 
Page 11 
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
The quantification of GHG reductions for Measure TR-1 is based on a reduction in VMT from an expansion of, and 
increased compliance with, commute trip reduction requirements under Zoning Ordinance 3050 Transportation 
Demand Management Ordinance. The reduction quantification assumes a 20% alternative mode (non-single-
occupancy vehicle) would be achieved for businesses required to comply with the updated ordinance, and that 
approximately 72% of business in Oceanside would have over 20 employees and be expected to comply with the 
requirements. GHG reduction estimates are likely underestimated, as baseline data assumes that approximately 15% 
of trips in the region are already by a mode other than single-occupancy vehicle; however, this does not account for 
commute trips to and work only, and considers travel to all destinations.

Measure TR-2: Enhance transit facilities and support accessibility to increase transit 
ridership by 4% by 2030 and 5% by 2045 from 2018 levels.3 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
Through this measure, the City will encourage increased transit ridership to shift travel from single-occupancy 
vehicles to public transit. The City will update current parking requirements by adopting ordinances to unbundle 
parking costs from property costs in new developments in transit priority areas. The City also plans to partner with 
regional transportation agencies such as NCTD and SANDAG to improve the efficiency of the transit system, 
providing more transit options, and making transit more accessible to everyone. To provide information to the 
3 North County Transit District’s ridership data collection methods changed in the 2019 fiscal year, and as such future tracking of transit ridership changes from a 
2018 baseline may need to consider these data collection changes.  


Oceanside CAP Update GHG Reductions Memo 
May 31, 2024, revised October 28, 2025 
Page 12 
 
community regarding these actions and understand the barriers and challenges the community faces, the City also 
plans to conduct outreach to encourage use of the transit system.  
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
The quantification of GHG reductions for Measure TR-2 is based on a shift in trips from passenger vehicles to transit. 
The reduction in VMT equates to a direct reduction in fuel consumption in on-road passenger vehicles, thereby 
reducing citywide GHG emissions. The key actions that are expected to drive GHG reductions were quantified using 
the California Air Pollution Control Officers Association (CAPCOA) Handbook for Analyzing Greenhouse Gas Emission 
Reductions, Assessing Climate Vulnerabilities, and Advancing Health and Equity Measure T-26 (CAPCOA 2021) and 
historical ridership data from implementation of SANDAG Flexible Fleets programs.

Measure TR-3: Increase zero emission vehicle (ZEV) adoption to 20% by 2030 and 90% by 2045 
through charging infrastructure deployment. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
Through this measure, the City aims to increase ZEV adoption by planning for and improving the EV charging 
infrastructure in Oceanside to reduce emissions generated by driving gasoline- and diesel-fueled vehicles. The City 
plans to adopt ordinances to require EV charging facilities at new and modified gas stations and commercial centers, 
as well as auxiliary power at delivery docks for reduced truck idling. The City will also develop a strategy to transition 
the municipal fleet to ZEVs by 2045. To support the community in the transition to ZEVs the City will promote 
incentives offered by various agencies. Measure TR-3 is intended to support and exceed the implementation of the 
State’s ZEV sale requirements of the Advanced Clean Cars II and Advanced Clean Fleets rules. 
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
GHG reductions for Measure TR-3 are quantified for the years 2045 and 2050 as the City will plan for the 
infrastructure needed to support a ZEV fleet of 90% of all vehicles. For 2030, the City is not expected to exceed State-
projected ZEV fleet targets of the Advanced Clean Cars II and Advanced Clean Fleets rules, which are accounted for in 
the ABAU GHG emissions forecast. GHG reductions for the years 2045 and 2050 are additional to those accounted 
for in the ABAU GHG emissions forecast.

Measure TR-4: Increase active transportation infrastructure to provide safe alternative 
options to vehicle travel, installing or upgrading 26 miles of bike lanes/paths and sidewalks 
by 2030 and 50 miles by 2045. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
Through this measure, the City plans to improve Oceanside’s active transportation infrastructure by increasing and 
improving bike lanes, sidewalks, and trails citywide and provide more opportunities to reduce emissions generated by 
driving vehicles. The City will accomplish this by implementing the existing Bicycle Master Plan, as well as developing 
and implementing a new Active Transportation Plan and Trails Master Plan, following adoption of the GPU. The City 
will also develop a bikeshare program and work with the Oceanside Unified School District to implement the Safe 
Routes to School Plan. The measure reflects the City's intention to reduce vehicular emissions while ensuring that all 
residents have a safe alternative to vehicle travel and can benefit from improved mobility and quality of life. 
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
The quantification of GHG reductions for Measure TR-4 is based on an expected mode shift from passenger vehicles to 
active transportation trips citywide. The GHG reductions were quantified based on the miles of active transportation 
infrastructure to be installed, according to the CAPCOA Handbook for Analyzing Greenhouse Gas Emission Reductions, 
Assessing Climate Vulnerabilities, and Advancing Health and Equity (CAPCOA 2021). The quantification guidance for 
measure T-20 in the CAPCOA guidance was used to quantify reductions for Measure TR-4.

Measure TR-5: Transition non-road and landscaping equipment in the City to electric and 
renewable fueled. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
Through this measure, the City aims to transition City-owned and -operated non-road equipment and community 
landscaping equipment to electric or renewable fuels by 2030. The City will update its procurement standards to 
preferentially purchase only electric-powered off-road and landscaping equipment and to provide preference to 
contractors that utilize renewable or electric-fueled equipment. To support the community in this transition, the City 
will create trade-in events for landscaping equipment and encourage the community to use available incentives, 
which further supports the State’s efforts of transitioning to all electric-landscaping equipment. 
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
The quantification of GHG reductions for Measure TR-5 is based on an expected reduction in fuel consumption in off-
road and landscaping equipment citywide. The GHG reductions were quantified based the elimination of fossil-fueled 
landscaping equipment citywide by 2030, with GHG reductions continued through 2045 and 2050 due to continued 
avoided emissions.

Measure TR-6: Incentivize infill development through implementation of smart growth 
policies in the City’s General Plan Update and Coast Highway Incentive District, such that 
60% of new residential units and commercial floor space are built within these plan areas 
through 2045. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
The City’s in progress General Plan Update includes numerous policies that focus on smart growth principles and are 
intended to support and incentivize growth and mobility in priority infill areas. This concerted effort across multiple 
general plan elements to ensure residents and workers in Oceanside can perform daily activities more efficiently, and 
with less reliance on vehicle travel, is essential for the City to reduce per-capita VMT and meet local GHG reduction 
targets. The General Plan elements that include policies contributing to this vision for Oceanside include: 
 
Efficient and Compatible Land Use (ECLU) Element, 
 
Integrated Mobility (IM) Element, and 
 
Smart and Sustainable Corridors Specific Plan (SSCSP). 
These in progress General Plan elements are in addition to the Coast Highway Vision and Strategic Plan, which 
includes multiple elements that strive for smart growth principles. These include the Coast Highway Corridor to 
perform complete streets upgrades to the Coast Highway Corridor Design complete streets projects, and the Coast 
Highway Incentive District (CHID). The overall goal is to centralize new development along the Coast Highway 
Corridor and create a more walkable and accessible environment for residents, workers and visitors.  
The combination of these General Plan elements and plans provides the City with a growth framework that prioritizes 
infill and neighborhood scale developments with complete streets principles that enable access to existing transit and 
essential services through walking, biking, and micro mobility options. The City intends to incentivize development in 
these priority areas by streamlining the development review process for projects that align with form-based 
standards and include community benefit features.  
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
Measure TR-6 is core to the success of the CAP Update and meeting GHG reduction targets, as the quantification of 
GHG reductions from VMT reductions is included in the VMT modeling for the CAP Update. The VMT estimates used 
to develop the GHG emissions forecast incorporate the land use scenario from the ECLU element, as well as 
implementation of policies included in the IM, SSCSP, and CHID. As such, successful implementation of the policies 
included in these plans and elements are inherently already quantified in the City’s trajectory to meeting GHG 
reduction targets of the CAP Update and separate GHG reductions are not quantified here for this Measures TR-1. 
Although this measure is not quantified here, it is important to note that GHG reductions are expected to be achieved 
from implementation of this measure is core to the success of the CAP Update.

Action TR-6.8 
By 2035, complete the Coast Highway Corridor Design Complete Streets Projects to enable safe and efficient bike and 
pedestrian travel along the Coast Highway Corridor. (Key) 
Action TR-6.9 
Prioritize capital improvements that support infill and redevelopment within the City's urban corridors, consistent with 
policies in the adopted SSCSP, with a focus on areas supportive of smart growth and active transportation. (Supportive) 
Action TR-6.10 
Continue to pursue grant funding for capital improvements that provide infrastructure to facilitate growth within the 
City’s urban corridors, including the SSCSP and CHID planning areas. (Supportive) 
WATER 
The Water sector of emissions represents a small proportion of the City’s total emissions, only 1% in 2018; however, 
the City has significant control over this emissions source through the ability to utilize local water supplies, reduction 
in demand, and low-carbon electricity sources for pumping, treating, and delivering water to customers. 
Measure W-1: Reduce per-capita water consumption in the City by 6% by 2030, continued 
through 2045, compared to 2018 levels. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
Through this measure, the City aims to reduce per-capita water consumption and hence reduce the upstream 
electricity emissions associated with the water sector by implementing a Water Conservation Plan, City Facilities 
Water Efficiency Plan, and continuing enforcement of the City’s Model Water Efficient Landscape Ordinance 
(MWELO). Through actions like water-efficient building practices, low-water landscaping, plumbing code updates, 
and outreach efforts, the City plans a phased reduction in per-capita water consumption by 2045. Additionally, the 
City plans on adopting ordinances to require water-efficient fixtures in new renovated buildings, distributing water 
conservation kits to low-income families, promoting water disclosure programs for businesses, and installing low-
water use landscaping in City-owned buildings. To support the community, the City plans to continue offering 
rebates on water-efficient fixtures and use Green Oceanside for maintaining outreach efforts.  


Oceanside CAP Update GHG Reductions Memo 
May 31, 2024, revised October 28, 2025 
Page 19 
 
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
The quantification of GHG reductions for Measure W-1 is based on an expected reduction in upstream electricity use 
as local water demand is reduced. The GHG reductions were quantified based on the water consumption reductions 
of Action W-1.1 and Action W-1.3. The reductions were based on a 2018 per-capita water consumption rate of 45,790 
gallons per year. In 2045 and 2050, no additional reductions are seen from implementation of this measure, as all 
retail electricity in the state is expected to be 100% GHG-free, consistent with RPS minimum requirements.

information about the garden on the City’s website. (Supportive) 
Measure W-2: Increase local water supply to provide 50% of City’s total water demand from 
local sources by 2030. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
Through this measure, the City aims to reduce emissions associated with the water sector and strengthen water supply 
infrastructure in Oceanside by increasing production from the Pure Water Oceanside project, expanding recycled water 
production at the San Luis Rey Water Reclamation Facility (SLRWRF), and promoting recycled water use through cost 
incentives. The measure intends to foster water resilience for all residents, ensuring access to a sustainable water supply, 
and potentially mitigating disparities in water availability and affordability across diverse communities.

GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
The quantification of GHG reductions for Measure W-2 is based on an expected reduction in upstream electricity use as 
more energy-intensive imported water is replaced with local water supply sources. The GHG reductions were quantified 
based on a transition from 89% of the total water supply being imported in 2020 to 50% of the total water supply being 
imported in 2030. In 2045 and 2050, no additional reductions are seen from implementation of this measure, as all retail 
electricity in the state is expected to be 100% GHG-free, consistent with RPS minimum requirements.

SOLID WASTE 
The Solid Waste sector of emissions represents a small proportion of the City’s total emissions, only 4% in 2018; with 
emissions resulting from the decay of organic materials in landfills over time, and energy used for landfilling 
operations. The emissions reduction measures and actions presented here focus on diverting waste from landfills 
through increased composting capacity, food waste recovery, and overall waste diversion. 
Measure SW-1: Achieve a 90% diversion rate for solid waste in landfills by 2030. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
Through this measure, the City intends to reduce the disposal of compostable organic materials to landfills by 
updating the City’s Zero Waste Plan, expanding the backyard composting program, and expanding the Zero Waste 
Schools Program. Planned actions include mandating organics collection for commercial customers and in-house 
composting for large restaurants, educating low-income households on food waste, incentivizing composting, and 
fostering school partnerships. The measure aims to reduce solid waste emissions while prioritizing environmental 
conservation. Through targeted outreach and participation incentives, the City intends to achieve inclusivity in its 
waste reduction efforts. 
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
The quantification of GHG reductions for Measure SW-1 is based on an expected reduction in landfilled organic 
waste, thereby reducing its contribution to methane emissions from landfills. The GHG reductions were quantified 
based on achieving a 90% diversion rate of organic waste, increased from the 67% diversion rate in 2018.

Measure SW-2: Continue implementing City recycling and inorganic solid waste programs to 
increase diversion beyond 2018 baseline levels. 
MEASURE SUMMARY 
Through this measure, the City aims to enhance recycling efforts and reduce the landfilling of inorganic waste in 
Oceanside. The City plans to accomplish this by extending North County waste management services, organizing "fix-
it clinics" to encourage repair and reuse, adopting a construction and demolition (C&D) recycling ordinance to 
require diversion of C&D waste, and exploring construction and demolition reuse initiatives. For successful 
implementation and to support the community throughout the process, the City plans to provide targeted education 
support and technical assistance to diverse sectors of the community to reduce waste and encourage better waste 
management through Green Oceanside. The City plans to hold community outreach events at locations most 
convenient to residents and in an easy-to-understand manner, ensuring all residents and businesses participate in 
and benefit from the measure. 
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
GHG reductions are not quantified for Measure SW-2, as it is not expected that a reduction in inorganic waste would 
achieve significant reductions in landfill methane emissions. However, this measure provides community benefits by 
reducing the amount of waste sent to landfill overall and provides resources for increased compliance with C&D 
diversion requirements.

CARBON SEQUESTRATION 
Enhancing and protecting the carbon sequestration potential of natural lands and the urban forest in Oceanside are 
key to supporting the State’s “30X30” initiative to protect 30% of the state’s natural lands and coastal waters by 2030. 
Relying on natural solutions to mitigate climate change can also enhance the City’s ability to adapt to potential 
climate change impacts. 
Measure CS-1: Plant 200 new trees (net) citywide, to achieve 800 new trees (net) by 2030, 
and 3,800 by 2045 while maintaining the existing urban tree canopy.  
MEASURE SUMMARY 
Through this measure, the City plans to sequester carbon through tree planting in Oceanside. The City plans to do 
this by adopting the Green Street Policy and encouraging tree planting on private property. The City plans to develop 
stringent compensatory measures for tree removal during construction in order to avoid or minimize tree removal or 
plan the appropriate replacement of removed trees. The City also plans to establish a living Tree Fund for financing 
future tree planting activities, acquired from new development projects not meeting the appropriate tree canopy 
standards. The measure aims to enhance environmental sustainability and health benefits for the community.  
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
GHG reductions for Measure CS-1 were quantified based on an increase in annual carbon sequestration from the 
planting of native trees using the i-Tree software suite from the United States Department of Agriculture (USDA) 
Forest Service. The quantification assumes 800 net new trees planted by 2030 and 3,800 by 2045.

Measure CS-2: Preserve agricultural lands and promote regenerative agriculture programs to 
increase carbon sequestration capacity of 100 acres of farmland by 2030.  
The City plans to preserve agricultural lands and implement a regenerative agriculture program to enhance carbon 
sequestration on agricultural lands in Oceanside. The plan involves streamlining permits for urban farming and 
collaborating with regional partners to develop and implement preservation program strategies. Under this measure, 
the City is also planning to promote Regenerative Soil Management Practices through strategic partnerships with the 
Mission Resource Conservation District and County of San Diego and support agricultural landowners/operators by 
providing an affordable, local source of mulch and compost. Through this program, the City plans to directly engage 
with stakeholders to share information and understand local compost/mulch needs. For this, the City plans on 
engaging with diverse stakeholders, providing resources to farmers, and promoting research opportunities through 
actions such as outreach, collaboration, and policy support. 
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
GHG reductions were not quantified for Measure CS-2 as sufficient data on current agricultural practices in Oceanside 
and the resulting scope of a regenerative agriculture program are not currently available.

LOCAL GREEN ECONOMY 
Encouraging residents and visitors to shop locally and supporting local job growth can reduce local and global GHG 
emissions, as well as supporting the initiatives of the Economic Development Element. Due to the complex interaction 
between local consumer choices and global supply chain emissions the measure and actions presented here are not 
quantified for local GHG reductions in the CAP Update. 
Measure LGE-1.1: Sustain local growth through providing green job opportunities to residents 
and promoting local businesses. 
With this measure, the City aims to promote sustainable local growth and equity through a multifaceted approach. 
The City plans to take initiatives including partnering with regional educational institutions to develop a local 
workforce equipped with new skills, documenting and promoting green practices in local businesses, assisting 
businesses to apply for sustainability grants, and educating the community on environmentally conscious 
consumption. The City also intends to promote local green businesses by educating Oceanside residents and visitors 
on the environmental benefits of buying locally produced goods. The City plans to use its own social media accounts 
and campaigns, including through partnerships with Visit Oceanside and the Chamber of Commerce. Overall, with 
the focus on developing a skilled local workforce for a low carbon economy and collaboration with educational 
institutions, the City intends to enhance local workforce skills, promote local businesses, and reduce GHG emissions 
associated with consumer products. 
GHG QUANTIFICATION APPROACH 
GHG reductions were not quantified for Measure LGE-1 as emissions reductions that would be achieved from this 
measure are outside of the boundary of Oceanside’s GHG emissions inventory.

Through a future Sustainable Business recognition program:  
(i) document innovative sustainable systems practiced by locally owned businesses, 
(ii) promote local green businesses through the City's social media accounts and publications. (Supportive) 
Action LGE-1.3 
Dependent on a dedicated funding source, partner with the Chamber of Commerce to develop a program that assists 
local businesses with submitting grant applications for sustainability related initiatives that can help expand their 
operations, such as circular economy initiatives. (Supportive) 
Action LGE-1.4 
Implement an educational campaign highlighting the environmental benefits of supporting local businesses, partnering 
with the Chamber of Commerce to maintain a list of businesses that sell locally produced goods (e.g., surfboards 
manufactured in Oceanside, restaurants using locally sourced produce). As part of the campaign, work with Visit 
Oceanside and hospitality establishments in the downtown district to provide suggestions to visitors for supporting 
locally produced goods. (Supportive)

Continue educational campaigns through Green Oceanside to provide information about sustainable consumption 
practices and how everyday activities, such as grocery shopping and online purchases, can be done more sustainably. 
(Supportive) 
Action LGE-1.6 
Support the continuation and expansion of urban agriculture in the City with implementation of HLC policies 7-8 
through 7-18. (Supportive)

Reduce per-capita vehicle miles traveled (VMT) 3% by 2030 and 6% by 2045 from 2016 levels through transportation demand management (TDM) strategies. 
Action TR-1.1
By 2027, amend the Transportation Demand Management Ordinance (Zoning Ordinance 3050) to require businesses that generate 40 or more daily commute trips to comply with 
the ordinance. Zoning Ordinance 3050 will also be amended to be applicable to businesses that generate 40 or more daily commute trips in redevelopment projects and at sites 
where a conditional use permit is being issued/updated.

Support proposed GP policy IM 4-70, 4-72 and 4-73 by partnering with SANDAG to implement the Flexible Fleets Implementation Strategic Plan in Oceanside.
Enhance transit facilities and support accessibility to increase transit ridership by 4% by 2030 and 5% by 2045 from 2018 levels.
Support proposed GP policy IM 4-69 by dedicating funds through the Public Works Department to implement transit-supportive treatments on 25% of transit routes by 2045, 
with a priority on connecting the main corridors in the SSCSP. Treatments can include transit signal priority, bus-only signal phases, queue jumps, curb extensions to speed 
passenger loading, and dedicated bus lanes. The improvements would be planned and implemented in coordination with NCTD, and the City would advocate for cost sharing 
among the agencies.

Consider adoption of an ordinance to prohibit the sales and use of gas-powered leaf blowers and lawn equipment within Oceanside, starting in 
2027. 
Note: Assumes all fuel consumption is gasoline, ackknowledging that construction equipment is likely to use diesel fuel. This assumption is not 
expected to substantially effect GHG reduction calculations, as fuel energy content and GHG emissions are directly related to the carbon content of 
fuels, and electricity will be supplied by 100% renewable electricity by 2045.

Reduce per-capita vehicle miles traveled (VMT) 3% by 2030 and 6% by 2045 from 2016 levels through transportation demand management (TDM) strategies. 
Action TR-1.1
By 2027, amend the Transportation Demand Management Ordinance (Zoning Ordinance 3050) to require businesses that generate 40 or more daily commute trips to comply with 
the ordinance. Zoning Ordinance 3050 will also be amended to be applicable to businesses that generate 40 or more daily commute trips in redevelopment projects and at sites 
where a conditional use permit is being issued/updated.

TR-2.8 GHG emissions reductions (MTCO2e)
-
                                                   
15
                           
6
                                
4
                               
Support proposed GP policy IM 4-70, 4-72 and 4-73 by partnering with SANDAG to implement the Flexible Fleets Implementation Strategic Plan in Oceanside.
Enhance transit facilities and support accessibility to increase transit ridership by 4% by 2030 and 5% by 2045 from 2018 levels.
Support proposed GP policy IM 4-69 by dedicating funds through the Public Works Department to implement transit-supportive treatments on 25% of transit routes by 
2045, with a priority on connecting the main corridors in the SSCSP. Treatments can include transit signal priority, bus-only signal phases, queue jumps, curb extensions 
to speed passenger loading, and dedicated bus lanes. The improvements would be planned and implemented in coordination with NCTD, and the City would advocate for 
cost sharing among the agencies.

Appendix C 
Survey Results


 
 
Online Survey Results 
Local Climate Mitigation 
 
The Onward Oceanside Project Team thanks the nearly 470 community members who participated in this 
survey.  Responses will help to inform the updating of the City’s Climate Action Plan. 
While a significant majority of survey respondents agree that climate change is a human-induced 
phenomenon that should be addressed, other respondents remain either skeptical or uncertain about the 
extent to which human activity is contributing to climate change. 
A high percentage of respondents indicate that they are mindful of their carbon footprint and make a 
conscientious effort to conserve energy and water, choose sustainable products, and minimize their solid 
waste stream.  At the same time, many respondents find that their choices are constrained by high costs, 
limited access, and a lack of reliable information.  Those who rent their homes or live in HOA-governed 
neighborhoods feel particularly constrained in terms of what they can do to reduce their energy and water 
consumption and/or source renewable power. 
Most respondents are highly dependent on the private automobile, and less than 30 percent own an 
alternative fuel vehicle.  Few survey respondents have calculated their carbon footprint. 
Most respondents are open to local energy efficient standards that exceed state standards, as long as 
compliance with these standards is affordable and cost-effective for local households and businesses.  
Although most respondents appear to agree that each of us should contribute to a collective effort to reduce 
GHG emissions, others express a wariness of regulation and assert their right to make choices that they 
believe to be in the best interest of their families and businesses.  
A key takeaway from the survey responses is that more community outreach on climate change and climate 
mitigation strategies would contribute to local emissions reduction.
